Summary

Leon Kennedy and Ada Wong navigating their relationship and learn that even when everything seems clear, there are still challenges to overcome. Challenges that can either break you or make you stronger.

This is a sequel to Shelter but it can also be read separately.

Chapter 1: Liminal

„Leon?“

He flinched a little at being addressed. He hadn’t expected anyone at this hour.

He had worked long enough to finish what had been mandatorily required. Still hadn’t managed to catch up with everything. And usually, he didn’t leave things undealt with, didn’t leave reports half-reviewed, didn’t leave emails unanswered. At least not the important ones. But the perspective on importance had started to shift. All he wanted at this point was to get out of here.

He paused stuffing his things into his bag and turned around. Relief washed over him at the sight. Maybe even some sense of joy. He was always happy to see her. His lips formed a smile that was immediately mirrored by one of her own.

“Hey” he said. “Hey there.” She glanced at his things. “Am I interrupting?” He shook his head. “No.” A lie. “I was just about to leave” he thus added quickly. Uhm, too bad though.” His frown deepened. “What’s wrong?” “Nothing, it’s just that… Well. I know I am late with this, but Saturday is my birthday. Just coming off the call with him, in fact. I haven’t seen him in a while.”

“Looks like lately D.C. is losing its grip on you” she observed. She gave him a soft earnest smile. “I like it. I like how you are lately. A lot. You smile more.” He just allowed that, didn’t answer. Sherry studied him for a second, a little cautious now. But no less curious. “I just wonder what it is.”

Rebecca knew something, of course. But apart from telling her when Ada had left a couple of months ago, he hadn’t gotten into further detail with her about it. Also, he hadn’t heard from her since then.

He looked away, jaw tightening just slightly. Because he knew exactly what she meant. “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” he defended, lacking all conviction. Her eyes bored into his. But she let it go. “Okay then. In case you happen to change your mind, come to my place on Saturday at eight.”

He just shrugged, less nonchalant rather uncertain. Because what was he supposed to say? He had absolutely no intention of staying in D.C. over the weekend. She accepted it. Just turned around, about to leave him alone. He huffed, didn’t see any sense in denial anymore. “Yeah, well. Thanks.” “Drive safe.” “Will do. I’ll call you on Saturday.” He put on his jacket, grabbed his things and left.

And keeping his DC apartment would have, in his eyes, been an unnecessary half measure. Thus, he had given notice to his lease and relocated all his belongings to their new home in Boston. Now he lived with her there, at the weekends. During the week he usually had to be in DC. Initially, at times when she was away, he had also stayed there in his off time. That, too, had meanwhile changed, however. He always returned home when his presence in the capital was no longer required.

But something about the encounter with Sherry confused him, gave him a strange uneasy feeling, something he didn’t know what. He pondered as he drove. It wasn’t the fact that he wouldn’t be able to make it to her birthday party. He didn’t mind at all and he knew she didn’t take it personally. He knew they would always be on good terms. So what was the problem?

He passed by an AT&T commercial, read from the corner of his eye as he drove way too fast. “It Can Wait”. The underlying picture showed two realities, one where someone texted and crashed and one where they didn’t. And suddenly that struck him. Not because the content matched his current situation but because it still somehow pointed something out. One life, two realities. One had continued as Agent Kennedy who was scraping by in a scarce one room studio in DC downtown. The realization came slowly. Just a thought at first, a passing idea he didn’t fully latch onto. But it soon became more distinct. If he had no intention of hiding, why not make it more official? Probably not much, he assumed. He had never disclosed anything more than the necessary about Ada. He had mentioned in his report about the Raccoon City incident that he had been helped in the labs by a stranger. He hadn’t mentioned her name though. He also hadn’t mentioned that he had been tricked by her and used to obtain a G-Virus sample. All that information wouldn't have made him look very good, and he had been embarrassed by it. Besides, he had thought she had been dead and the virus lost anyway.

He had mentioned the involvement of a spy in his report on Spain. Mainly because Ashley Graham had picked up on a lot that he couldn't have covered up. However, he had claimed not to know exactly who she had worked for or what her real name had been. Which, at that point, hadn’t even been much of a lie.

He had left her out of his report on Tall Oaks and Lanshiang entirely. Helena Harper hadn’t opposed at all. Just signed off what he had written after reading without making any comment. Chris had mentioned everything he’d thought he’d known about her in his debrief, however. Which had been shit. But he expected the name Ada Wong to thus at least ring a bell. Even if it would be the wrong one.

He briefly wondered about Ada’s involvement in NEST, about Annette Birkin. But Ada hadn’t attacked her back in the days, it had been the other way around. So, from a solely technical point of view, there was nothing essential standing between her and Sherry.

And what would it change anyway? She had worked for Wesker, yes, and he knew it wouldn’t sit well with his friends. Like it still didn’t sit well with him. Which was an understatement. But she had never hurt any of them. And he knew who he belonged to. He had spent what felt like a lifetime longing for this one person. And now she was there and he had never been more certain about, more convinced of anything. There was no fucking room for doubts.

The small lamp shedding low, diffused light instead of illuminating everything. His large dining table. Everything that had never felt warm or personal to him was still there.

And did so now. Because now there were cushions on his couch. Not many. Not thrown there. Placed. Folded once, casually. The coffee table was no longer empty. There was a small stack of books. Biographies, nonfiction, novels. Not perfectly aligned but slightly offset. Even if I can’t lose what I didn’t have in the first place.” For a long moment Ada held her gaze, dark eyes endlessly deep. There was a quiet understanding in them. An understanding that didn’t quite make sense. Like her statement earlier hadn’t. She doubted Leon had been hard to address for her. He was quite approachable. Genuinely kind. She thought twice.

“Not a long time ago, I would have advised you to let it go” Ada said suddenly. “But now, I’d say let him know what you want him to know. He can take it. I cannot predict what he will make of it. But I think, after all, that speaking your truth is worth it. Some people need to hear what they think they long knew.” She blinked at her. That hadn’t been the response she had expected. It felt like a confession.

“And what do I say?” Ada chuckled slightly. “Well, perhaps I wouldn’t open with something very heavy” she teased lightly. It made her smile. “Never. Not if I don’t want him to run away immediately.” She smiled a little defeated and disorientated smile to herself. “Tell him that you are happy to see him. Ask him how he has been and endure silence when it is all he has to offer. Be present without taking his space.” Ada shook her head, cut herself off as if she feared it had already been said too much. “But what do I know. I am a horrible consultation in his regard anyway.”

She didn’t think so at all. Quite on the contrary, it silenced most of her still lingering doubts, made her feel strangely fond of her. She locked gazes with Ada for a long moment, didn’t let go. “I listen, Ada. Even though I have to admit that probably nobody in this room would approve of your advice” she said. Ada remained perfectly calm, entirely unsurprised. “I am used to not being approved of.” At that her brows shot up. She wanted to ask a million questions, was bordering on bringing up Edonia. But she bit her lip instead, because the look she suddenly received from Ada was deep and guarded for once. And she knew that this simply wasn’t the time. Knew that if she crossed a line now, she’d driver her away forever. And she didn’t want that. She relaxed her posture, Ada relaxed in response, still surveying her, however. She broke the silence, moving back to safer even if equally pressing terrain. “He's so different from me. There are so many things we disagree on. So much so that I sometimes wonder if it even makes sense.” “Someone recently taught me that what makes sense isn’t always what you need.” Ada’s gaze shifted towards Leon, who now stood across the room with Claire.

Her breath caught because that was it. Exactly it. And it was all she had needed to hear. She exhaled slowly, the knot in her chest loosening slightly. “Yeah. I agree.”

Ada looked back at her. “You’ll figure it out.” She returned her gaze. Noticed the strange gravity she felt in front of that woman, in front of Leon’s girlfriend. She had no idea what she had been through with him, on second thought rather what he had been through with her. But that she was here now, beside her, attending her party with him gave her a strange kind of hope. And at the same time an indescribable feeling towards Ada. She could already tell they would get along. Whatever had happened in the past, today they were aligned.

With Leon briefly occupied and anything resembling privacy otherwise nonexistent, she quickly excused herself and took the opportunity to retreat to the bathroom to take a breath. She avoided looking at anybody as she crossed the apartment and finally reached her destination. She slipped inside, felt for the light switch.

And then then door slammed open from behind her. Wood cracked hard against her shoulder and threw her off balance, driving her forward to the sink. Pain flared immediately but she bit it back as her instincts kicked in. She twisted, her mind unable to catch up, unable to assess what was happening. But she recognized Jake Muller through the haze of adrenaline, inside the room, already closing the distance. His hand shot to her again, most likely aiming to pin her against the counter. She couldn’t react fast enough, she was still recovering from the previous hit. He was going for control and she didn’t move for an instant, let him think he had it.

For half a second before she set herself in motion. She let her weight drop, shoulder rolling through the lingering impact as she redirected his grip, turning his forward drive into a glancing collision with the sink instead of her. Porcelain rattled under the force, the mirror shuddering behind it. He adjusted instantly though, forcing the space closed again, one arm bracing against the counter to box her in. Easy to read though, he was too direct, too convinced of himself. She had seen those men. And stepped in instead of away. Her hand snapped up, catching his wrist, twisting just enough to break alignment. She knew his strengths wouldn’t allow for her to stop him but she could disrupt what he was trying. Her other hand moved just as fast, sliding across the counter, feeling around for something and finding the coiled charging cable beside the toothbrush. Her lips tugged upwards all by themselves.

He shoved forward again, trying to overpower the angle, but she pivoted, slipped past his centerline. The cable looped around his neck as she moved, her motion carrying her behind him before he could fully react. She pulled back. Not very hard. She still was painfully aware of where she was, who he was. But enough. The tension set immediately and Jake Muller stilled, breath tightening for a fraction of a second as the line went taut. Not enough to choke. Plenty to remind.

“…You might fool the goodie-goodies” he rasped through poor breath, voice low, edged with fury while he tried to writhe “but not me.” She tightened her grip to force him to not move as she tried to sort through this. Why was he attacking her? She hadn't even realized that he had noticed her. Had she been careless? Before, they had never really met, had they? She had helped him and Sherry in Lanshiang. But she had been fast. Or so she had assumed. Perhaps not as fast as she had thought. Perhaps he had seen her. She had no clue though what the problem was. Well. On the upside this was a good opportunity to ask. “Some way of introducing yourself” she said, voice low against his neckline. “Most men offer me a drink if they want to get to know me.” She felt him tense. Tightened her grip again. “Funny. And here I thought we would have been past that” he choked. She furrowed her brows. Even if he had seen her when she had assisted them with this chainsaw bastard. Why would he be so pissed about it?

“Did you now? How’s that?” she replied calmly. A rough breath left him, edged with something darker than simple aggression. “Edonia ring a bell?” he asked and glanced at her through the mirror. “No man would forget a face like that.” It did ring a bell. Just not in the way he probably thought it did. “I have never been to Edonia” she answered flatly. He let out a short, humorless laugh, shifting slightly against the cable. She held her grip steady, noticed he also didn’t really struggle, just some testing.

“Oh yeah? Strange. Because there was someone with the very same face, the very same voice.” He exhaled sharply against the pressure at his throat. She let loose just a little bit. “Well, I am sorry having to disappoint since you seemed so happy about having run into me. But I would guess the person who you were looking to see was terminated by the BSAA already years ago.” Her voice dropped low. “Don’t take it too hard. You’re not the first to make that mistake.”

At that she felt him freeze at last. She assumed she had all of his attention now, didn’t speak, waited for him to make the next move. Which he did. “…So what” he said after a beat, tone shifting now to something more suspicious than aggressive “you’ve got a double running around? Someone who looks just like you?” He let out a huff-like laugh at the ridiculousness. She didn’t find it all too funny but met his gaze when he turned his head just enough to catch her in his peripheral.

For a moment, none of them moved. She looked at his face, caught herself surveying his exact features. She had seen him before, just not that close. Those eyes. It sent a shiver down her spine. She had never wanted to see those eyes again. But she couldn’t look away, let the cable loosen enough for him to be able to turn to her. She stood before him, staring up at these features. She clenched her jaw, watched his countenance tick to irritated. For a moment he seemed to survey her too.

“You look like you have seen a ghost.” She didn’t answer immediately. But she had stared at him for too long for him not to notice. So she nodded. “It kind of feels like it.” Jake stepped a little closer to her, she didn’t move. “And does that surprise you?” he asked, voice tense, a little testing now. She drew in a deep breath. “Surprise might not be the right way to put it. I’d rather say it reminds me.” His features hardened. “On an insane monster? That’s what you want to say?” She let out a huff. One could say that. He had been an insane monster. But she expected the world had told him that by now. And as she kept her eyes on him, she watched something pass behind them. Despise. Torment. She thought about the things that the world probably hadn’t told him, because how could they have? They hadn’t known him. Not really. Except for Chris Redfield, perhaps. “On a man I once knew.” She saw his eyes widen as she continued. “A man of whose defaults you surely heard plenty. And none of it is untrue. A heavy heritage for you, I’d assume. But… it isn’t all there was about him.” She thought about chance after chance he had given her. Thought about failed missions and next assignments. Thought about hard lessons, even harder training, violation, an iron grip and battles and complete dominance and respect and… She thought about Spain. About going astray for someone he had known she had grown fond of. He had been aware of this weakness of hers. He had seen it all the time. And yet… He hadn’t taken it away from her like he simply could have. He had not interfered. Not even when she had chosen that man over him. He had let her live. Had not gone after her like he could have. She had not expected to get away with it. And yet, she was still here. Standing before his son. Very much alive. She locked gazes with Jake, had no idea why she was telling him this right now, why she spoke at all. But perhaps it was because she suddenly felt like she owed it. Not to him. But to his father. “When I look at you I see the resemblance not only to a monster, but to a man that was precise, not just in execution but also in judgement. Intelligent in a way that often had me stunned in awe. He didn’t waste effort, he never rushed, understood leverage better than anyone else. He knew when to apply pressure, or when to hold back. He wasn’t careless. He valued results and control. I learned all these things from him.” He now studied her with eyes that had lost their hardness, something quieter replacing the earlier hostility. It wasn’t trust, neither acceptance. But perhaps curiosity. “…You’re giving him a lot of credit” he muttered. She arched a brow, didn’t disagree. “I’m not passing judgment. I just told you what I see because you asked me and because the rest of the world wouldn’t because they can’t because they don’t know.” “But you do?” She held his gaze. “Yes. I knew him.”

Silence fell, tension subsided. Eventually, she fully let go of the cable. She knew he wouldn’t attack anymore. He reached to rub his neck. Then he locked his gaze back on her.

“I believe you when you say we have never met” he suddenly said. She lifted her brows. “Is that so? What is it that made you change your mind? Softness just because I said something nice about your father? I didn’t say I wasn’t glad he is dead.” He didn’t flinch, didn’t blink, didn’t look away, ignoring her sharp edge entirely. “No. It’s the way you looked at me. You have never seen me before. There is no doubt.” She just gave a small nod, didn’t know what to say. It did strike her. Not in a positive way. But not entirely in a bad way either. Strangely she found that she kind of liked him. But then suddenly their current circumstances kicked back into her brain. She was in Sherry Birkin’s bathroom. With Jake Muller. The man Sherry had just about confessed to her she had feelings for. And Leon was out there. And even if she generally didn’t mind compromising situations, she had absolutely no intention of creating one tonight. And so she tilted her head towards the door.

“If you’re done then, I’d appreciate a moment to myself.” He didn’t move right away but started to approach the door after a beat. He paused before he reached the handle. “Good to meet you. Ada Wong. Whoever you are. I have a feeling this might have not been the last time.” She arched a brow, drew in a deep breath. “Muller?” she shot a look over her shoulder, nodding towards his neck. “Cover that up. You wouldn’t want to look like you have just attacked one of Sherry’s guests for no reason, would you?” He gave her a cunning smile in response. “Absolutely not. Also, we wouldn’t want our favorite superhero to get all upset, would we? I assume he would claim exclusivity when it comes to getting strangled by you.” She shot him an unimpressed look before she faced forward again. “Close the door behind you” was all she answered before she heard the door shut with a soft click. She checked the mirror, corrected her dress, put her hands on the sink to brace herself over it. What a fuckup of a night. All these silent stares, the analyzing. It was a surprise at this point that Jake had been the only one to attack her. She wondered who else Carla had encountered and who now thought they had seen her before. She was entirely not in control of the situation. And she hated not to be in control. Uncertainties were under preparation. And under preparation meant danger. Why had she agreed to do this in the first place? It felt so wrong. It had from the beginning. So wrong that she wanted to run. Like she always had when things had gotten too much to handle.

But what would happen if she did besides that it would kill him? She would lose the only place she had ever called home. She would lose slow mornings with shared coffees and tight embraces. She would lose little relaxed smiles over his shoulder when he was occupied with something. She would lose the only person she had ever loved.

She thought about Sherry. Had she seemed suspicious? The more she thought about it the more she came to the conclusion that the contrary had been the case. She had, in fact, been actually nice. Maybe way too nice even. But it hadn’t felt fake. Either because she didn’t know anything which she doubted. She expected her to at least have read through all the files on the Edonia and Lanshiang incident. Or because she had decided to give her a chance regardless. For Leon, perhaps. Most likely even.

She thought about his colleagues. They had seemed nice enough as well. And Claire… Well. She had avoided them apart from some occasional glances through narrowed eyes for which she couldn’t even blame her. Especially not after everything her brother had probably told her. And she, too, didn’t trust people easily. Especially not when everything looked so crystal clear from the outside. She realized she still held the cable, put it back to the rim. Strangely, she found her mind drifting back to Wesker. If there was one thing she had learned from him, it had been to not fall into a panic. To assess a situation before drawing conclusions. To control impulses. To follow logic instead of instinct. To think first. She forced herself to think logically, to not fall into old patterns just because it felt simpler. So all in all, perhaps she was overreacting at this point. Nothing had happened, she reminded herself. The tension she felt wasn’t their fault. Her old habits weren’t either. And wasn’t what she had with him worth standing her ground for? Was it even required for her to stand her ground at this point? Fuck. Whatever. She pressed her eyes shut, then reopened them. She sprinkled her face with some cold water, dried it off and decided to head back. There was no point in hiding.

He exchanged a few friendly glances with some of the guests as he made his way over to the opposite side of the room. It wasn’t a large number, but enough to decently fill the apartment. And he felt a little under surveillance as he walked. He briefly looked to where Jake Muller had stood before to find that he was heading somewhere, perhaps to the kitchen or the bathroom. He faced ahead, wondered if he should go talk to the guy. Thought he would after he had finished this. He couldn’t stand his passive aggressive restraint.

She was leaning against the wall next to the kitchen entry. Arms crossed before her chest, beer in her hand, gaze directed to the floor. He drew in a deep breath, slowing his pace, as he moved until he stood before her. She didn’t look up.

“Hi Claire” he offered. At that, her gaze snapped up. Piercing eyes locked at his own. Her expression was a mix between disbelief and anger. He clenched his jaw. Braced himself for anything. Not unexpectedly, she didn’t waste time with courtesies.

“What the fuck is she, Leon?” Usually, facing her in a mood like this would have caused somewhat of a distress within him. Now though, he realized with some surprise on his own side, he remained entirely at ease. His heartbeat was regular. His voice was steady as he answered. “Her name is Ada. She is my…” girlfriend, he had intended to say but paused instead. Paused to think because suddenly, calling her his girlfriend felt like an absurd understatement. Girlfriend sounded like something light. Something new. Something not necessarily permanent. And it wasn’t true for her. Wasn’t true for them. Wasn’t true for him. He swallowed, held Claires questioning gaze. “I love her” he rephrased and was a little shocked about the finality of his statement. He hadn’t even flinched at admitting what he had so carefully hidden, even from himself, for so long. She was no less startled, her eyes went wide, her jaw dropped a fraction. And for a second it rendered her speechless. She composed quickly, however.

“What? You cannot be fucking serious, Leon. Ada?” He could tell she was getting at something and had some good guesses about what it would be. But he wasn’t sure and he also had no intention of going on the defensive right away. There was no reason to do so. So he just nodded. “Yes, Ada. I would have gladly introduced you already, but I felt like you were avoiding me before.”

“Oh, I don’t think you have to, Leon. The moment you walked her through that door I knew who she was” she shot back, eyes flicking past him toward where he assumed Ada stood. “Ada Wong? Ada fucking Wong? You bring her here?” He kept his expression neutral, didn’t avoid her eyes. “Yes. I brought her here.” She stared at him like she was questioning his sanity. “The person responsible for the deaths of thousands of people? For the death of Chris entire squad in Edonia? Derek Simmons whore?” At that his eyes narrowed, his voice dipped low. “Careful, Claire. I will let you get away with saying something like that about her just this once. Because you don’t know any better.” They held each other’s gaze. “The reports say she is dead. Chris thinks she is dead. And now you show up with her here? You of all people? Have you even considered telling Chris? Telling all of us?”

He inhaled. “There was nothing to tell you. Like you said. Chris killed the person responsible for the death of his men. The person behind Lanshiang and Tall Oaks and Edonia. The business is finished.” She frowned then let out a fake laugh. “I see. And the fact that the woman you brought looks just like the perpetrator and has the same name is, of course, just a coincidence, right?” His posture was straight before her. “It is not a coincidence. But what has happened wasn’t done by Ada and it also wasn’t her responsibility. If anything, she helped me solve the matter.” Her mouth had fallen wide open. “And will you, by any chance, also be so kind as to explain this to me? Because at present, I find what you say pretty fucking hard to believe.”

Of course he could tell her. Of course he could give a persuading explanation for everything. But Ada had put her trust in him. She had shared things about her with him, trusting these things were safe with him. And he had no intention at all to betray her trust. Even if he trusted Claire, too. Even if he knew whatever he said was safe with her, irrespective of the terms on which they were personally. “Claire, listen. It wasn’t her. I know it for sure. I understand things are confusing. But I have known this woman for more than two decades. She saved my ass countless times. Without her, I wouldn’t be standing here anymore.”

She frowned deeply, briefly pausing to think. Then her eyes locked back on his. They hadn’t softened one bit. Perhaps even hardened. “So are you saying you knew? You knew who Ada Wong was before everything and didn’t help Chris? Didn’t tell him? Didn’t help at all to sort the mess out? What the hell is wrong with you, Leon? Were you compromised by the way she fucked you? Over – what was it again -two damn decades?” He ignored all her insults. Withstood her glare. Spoke utterly calmly now. “Yes, I knew her. But it doesn’t mean I was compromised. I looked into all the facts. I talked to her about everything. I made my assessment. And you can trust me when I say that it wasn’t her.”

She shook her head. “So, you say you looked into these things. But you left all of it out of the reports, didn’t you?” He had done that. Guilty as charged. He stood by it. Not sharing things Ada had trusted him with was another thing than lying to Claire. He had no intention of doing that. “Yes. I have. I protected her.” “And Harper just went along with it? Saw no wrong in everything? She was your partner!” He wouldn’t pull Helena into this, so he said nothing and they just stared each other down. Until something within her expression changed. Subtly but still there. She studied him more carefully now, searching for something he couldn’t know what. He returned her gaze, tried to force further open whatever had just cracked in her surface.

“Look Claire, I am sorry that this unclarity is dissatisfying. Believe me, I get that. But I have been through a lot with her. I questioned her. Questioned myself. And my silence hasn’t anything to do with the intention of hiding things from you, or Chris, or Sherry or anyone. But still that’s all I can tell you. That I know exactly who she is. And that I am asking you to trust my judgement.”

He was fully aware of the fact that she didn’t know she had been working for Albert Wesker as well. And he knew that if she learned about it, if any of them did, it would cause another stir. But he decided it didn’t matter at present. Not because he felt the need to hide it from them, not at all. But because disclosing things about her wasn’t his call to make. And because the issue was sufficiently solved between him and her and at this point nobody else’s business anymore. It made no difference anyway if they knew or not because even if they did, he’d stood by her side no matter what. And he needed no absolution for it.

Claire searched his face again, slower this time. “And do you trust your own judgement?” she asked. He nodded. “I trust her” he said. Not angry, but certain. And he meant it. After all these years he meant it. He had nothing left to prove. A long silence followed. Then her gaze softened just a fraction. It didn’t look like agreement, but perhaps resembled recognition. “… You’re in deep,” she said. He let out a quiet huff of breath. “Yeah” he admitted. Claire shook her head again, but this time it wasn’t just disbelief. It was closer to reluctance now. “…She better be worth it, Leon.” “She is” he said steadily.

They exchanged a long look before she nodded at least. He knew it wasn’t defeat, not just yet. “I would like to introduce you” he repeated and she held his gaze before she drew in a deep breath. “I am not sure about it.” He nodded, carefully. “I will not force this, Claire. But it would mean something to me.” The deep sigh he received in response was answer enough. He slowly turned around and started walking, felt she was following. Relief washed over him and he opened his side for her, let her fall into pace.

When they reached his colleagues and Sherry, she wasn’t there. He glanced around when his gaze got caught by Sherry who gave him a soft reassuring smile. “She excused herself to use the bathroom” she informed and he felt like weight was lifted from his mind and a moment later he caught sight of her. She was returning and he felt something in his chest settle just at seeing her. He gently placed his hand to her bare shoulder as she reached them, his thumb briefly brushing her skin before he forced himself to still. His heartbeat picked up as his eyes were met by hers. “Hey” he said in a tone softer than he had meant to sound with everyone else around listening closely. Ada’s eyes moved from him to Claire, her expression shifting slightly. Not to tension, not hostility. But perhaps awareness. “Hello” she said. He realized he had fallen silent, collected himself quickly.

Claire had straightened slightly beside him. He cleared his throat. “Claire, this is Ada.” He felt Claire’s attention sharpen. For a moment she just looked. “I thought I had seen you before” she said and he swallowed hard. Ada inclined her head slightly. “Oh. Then perhaps it might be the case.” There was no edge in her voice, he noticed. “He just told me it wasn’t” Claire added and he shot her a look that she ignored. He had no fucking clue why she was pulling that now and he already regretted having brought her over. About to put something in he got cut off by Ada who had remained motionless, completely neutral. “And you trust his judgement?” He blinked. Fuck, this was getting out of hand but he didn’t get a word in edgewise. “He asked me to.” Ada raised her brow. “That’s not an answer.”

Claire didn’t miss a beat. “It is” she said evenly. “Just perhaps not the one you were looking for.” He closed his eyes for half a second. Absolutely fantastic. Sherry, somewhere just behind Ada, had gone very, very quiet. He didn’t even need to look. He already felt the attention. Maya too. Most likely Frank. Probably half the room at this point. He prepared himself for anything. Ada’s tongue was sharper than a blade and delivered hits harder than a magnum. Impossible to parry for anyone.

Her eyes flickered to him for the fraction of a second before they returned to Claire and then she opened her mouth and he felt a shiver cascade down his spine. Here we go. But to his utter disbelief, she just nodded. “I see. Pleased to meet you in any case. Claire.” Claire frowned deeply. It earned her a sharp look from Sherry and he watched her expression ease a little in response. Then she nodded slightly, perhaps trying to convince herself. “Yeah. Me too.”

He felt it more than he was able to hide. The relief. Ada seemed to register it immediately because there was a slight hint of a shift in her posture, something quieter, more deliberate. She was keeping this low. She was doing it for him. And he couldn’t have been more glad. God he loved her so much for it. For everything. For coming here, for going through all these conversations tonight. She had done it just for him, endured this fuckup just because she knew it was important for him. He would make it up to her, later. For the rest of his life. He automatically stepped closer to her, felt her warmth. He would have wanted to put his arm around her shoulders, hug her tightly. But it felt too private a thing to do in this moment. He was already pushing her way too far tonight, further than he had ever intended to.

He tried to think of something to say when mercifully Sherry stepped in between him and Claire, giving them her most radiant smile. “Thank you all for coming tonight” she beamed. And then she looked at Ada. “It really means a lot having you here.” He watched Ada answer with a polite smile. Then Sherry picked up his gaze. “Even though I have to warn you” she proclaimed.

He exhaled slowly, running a hand through his hair. “Spare it. You already know I ignored all warnings I ever got” he muttered. Sherry laughed. And Claire did so, too, looking like she hadn’t intended to but hadn’t been able to hold it in. “No chance. Now you brought Ada into our lives, I might abduct her from you sometime for a coffee or something.” He watched Sherry’s expression soften into something that looked like fondness. And he melted at the sight, suddenly feeling so full, so full that he didn’t mind any longer that this particular conversation hadn’t gone too well, so full that he didn’t even quite know what to do with it. His breath stalled for half a second before he let it out slowly. And he felt a helpless smile pulling at his mouth before he could stop it. And for once he didn’t even try.

She listened to their talk for a while. They shared much and she hadn’t been a part of it and still had. She knew what Claire was referencing to when she thought she didn’t. She felt the bond between all of them, knowing what they had seen, what they had been through. She had spared a bullet or two here and there saving all of their heads and none of them, not even Leon, knew to which extent. She had so long loved him from the sidelines that this moment felt unsettlingly connected. Despite the fact that she remained an outsider. Despite the fact that she knew it would take a lot more to be accepted within their rows. Despite she wasn’t sure she even wanted to. But all of it made it very easy to ignore that Claire clearly did her best to leave her on the outside of the conversation. She didn’t blame her at all. And right now, she felt alright with having opted against making a run for it.

Suddenly he caught her gaze, pulled himself from the conversation to deeply look her in the eyes before he fully turned to her and took a step closer, so close that his thigh came to rest against hers. He didn’t reach, never presumptuous, but his sudden full presence was enough to send tickers down her spine. “Thank you for accompanying me tonight” he said, bending forward, voice low. She froze in place, held her breath. Then he inclined his head, brought his lips close to hers without fully leaning in and whispered “I love you.”

It wasn’t dramatic. It wasn’t reckless. But steady, unpolished, and entirely sincere. And it disarmed her more effectively than anything else ever had. She felt heat spread into every corner of her body, felt her heartrate pick up together with the strong urge to close what little distance was left between them to kiss him. She pulled together all of her resolve to hold herself back. “You clean up well, Kennedy” she murmured instead, never leaving his gaze and he huffed faintly, almost a smile. “I am afraid not just yet” he admitted and she arched a brow. “Oh?” “One more favor.” Her brow lifted. “You’re certainly pushing your luck today.” He nodded, not objecting. “I know. But I want to check on Muller” he said. “Anything else feels inappropriate. And you have to come with me. Because I have no intention of leaving you alone again tonight.” She bit back a smile tugging on her lips and followed his glance. After their little encounter in the bathroom Jake had apparently retreated exactly to where he had stood all night. Half-withdrawn, half-watching, like he still hadn’t decided if he wanted to be here in the first place. She sighed, didn’t wish to stir more fires for this night. But another look into Leons eyes told her that she wouldn’t get a saying in this anyway. He was too stubborn when he’d set his mind on something.

…Fine” she just said. “But you’re doing the talking.” He huffed, shook his head. “Absolutely not.” He started walking, leaving the rest behind without as much as another look and she just followed, glancing back over her shoulder. Sherry found her eyes immediately. She didn’t smile, didn’t gesture, just flickered her gaze towards the vast other side of the room. Sherry remained expressionless, just kept her eyes on hers until she faced forward again. Well. It was her turn now.

She focused her attention back to Leon as he had just reached Jake, in time to hear the tail end of his dry delivery. “—so if you were aiming for ‘mysterious and distant,’ congratulations. You nailed it.” She rolled her eyes and Jake exhaled through his nose. “Wasn’t aiming for anything.” “Sure” Leon said. “It just comes naturally.”

She stepped in beside them and both heads turned to her. Chapter 4: Allegiance

But it was a sharp contrast to the clear and defined air in the north where she had spent most of her time lately. For a moment, she focused on the faint sheer of humidity on her skin. Before red traffic lights while driving through D.C. after midnight when he was reminded of the way she had kissed him there once.

And generally, it would have been nothing extraordinary. Just an average containment site sweep. But he had been assigned a rookie analyst to the tactical unit under his lead. For experience. And even that hadn’t been too unusual. He was often entrusted with instructing inexperienced personnel. Because usually he was kind to his people, felt rather protective over young colleagues than thinking of them as a burden. He vividly remembered how he himself had felt on his first day on duty. Entirely overstrained. When they had been about to enter a dark room after hearing movement, however, the rookie had frozen for half a second. Had hesitated advancing. And normally he would have adjusted immediately, would have redirected calmly, would have simply covered the mistake without humiliating the boy for it. But this time, to his own utter bewilderment, he had snapped. Had shouted at him to move, sharp enough to make the whole radio channel go silent. The analyst had obeyed instantly, pale underneath his helmet, and he had simply pushed past him to clear the site like a fucking catharsis.

Afterwards during the debrief between armored vehicles beneath pouring rain, he had caught the younger agent avoiding eye contact with him entirely. Ingrid Hunnigan had called him later that day, privately, and carefully neutrally asked him if everything had been alright. He had snapped something and excused himself lying about another meeting. When he had returned to the office two days later, however, he had sought the young agent out and apologized for his behavior. And really, he retrospectively hadn’t had any clue anymore what had been wrong with him. Perhaps the lack of sleep, the long working hours, the pressure of the moment. When deep down inside he knew none of those factors had been decisive. He had functioned for all his life under not exactly ideal circumstances. He offered the man to train him though and to take him under his personal wing to make up for it. Which he from then on did.

The weekend after that it became even worse. After arriving home, he went straight to the bathroom, poured a decent amount of her shower gel in the tub and took a bath inhaling her scent to an extent that his head started to hurt. When he got out, still stupidly smelling like her, he went to the kitchen and stopped right in the middle of it, before the cooking island. It was suddenly so silent here. Had it always been like this for him before her? How had he endured it? This crushing void? It was enough to make his chest physically tighten and he realized painfully how accustomed he had become to another person inhabiting his life. Not just another person. The one he had loved forever. He walked over to the wide window front that faced their terrace and stared outside. Where are you Ada? Please come home.

By the middle of the sixth week the agitation started fully leaking through operationally. An investigation into missing black-market samples stalled after a lead in Virginia collapsed completely. Three days of surveillance went wasted and informants went silent. No traces left of the broker they had been tracking. And he lost patience during briefing.

“Just fucking find another angle” he said flatly after Theresa explained they had exhausted current intel channels. “We are trying….” she retorted but he cut her off. “No. What you do is waiting until something falls into your lap.” Brad, one of the senior agents he only rarely worked with and with who he had unglamorously failed that test back in days pushed back carefully. “Kennedy, we’ve been at this forty-eight hours straight.” “And?” he asked and saw the reaction afterwards. The tightening shoulders of the people around him. The way they exchanged looks. He dragged a hand down his face, closed his eyes. Jesus fucking Christ. It was hardly their fault. They were doing their best. He leaned both hands against the conference table and exhaled slowly through his nose. The anger drained almost as quickly as it had arrived, leaving only exhaustion behind. “I am sorry” he muttered. “You are doing your best. I know. I will look for possible ways to proceed myself.” Too bad he couldn’t ask Ada this time, however. He would have liked to knock his head against a wall. And he realized the embarrassing and merciless truth. This wasn’t about the case. This was about her. About her having vanished from his life again, leaving him with no way to reach her. It was like he had entirely lost his direction. And that wasn’t even the worst part. The worst part was that he had gotten used to having her so ridiculously quickly. So much that he now simply needed her around. It was so terribly lonely without her. He missed her kisses, her touch, fuck his whole body ached for the feel of her against his skin. And what he missed most was the way he could just tell her everything. The way he shared his life with her. The way she always understood. The way she listened to him. And while he had taken loneliness for granted for so long, she had relieved him of it and the pain it had brought in no time. And now it was threatening to catch up with him more forcefully than ever before. He was no stranger to addiction, yet this particular withdrawal hit so much harder than anything ever had before.

He rested his forehead into his palm, supported his skull with his arm. He didn’t even know if she was safe. And all his instincts suddenly commanded him to try and find her. He went back to his desk, started checking secure intelligence channels. For unusual activity reports. Missing persons clusters. Bioterror chatter moving through federal systems. Any mention of anything unusual. But he didn’t find one single issue that would have struck him as particularly interesting. He considered contacting Chris again but put that train of thought aside for the moment. He had repeatedly tried to reach him over the past weeks with no outcome and without ever being called back. He leaned back in his chair, folded his hands behind the back of his head. There was no fucking way he would track Ada Wong down anyway. He had tried that for decades before without any success. So, in the end, he just went back to his small apartment, grabbing takeout on the way, and let himself fall into the bed after unholily devouring his meal while still standing before the kitchenette counter.

Rain in Louisiana had a way of settling into everything. It wasn’t falling here. It was infiltrating. It clung to the air, to her skin, soaked the earth until roads dissolved into mud and entire stretches of forest smelled permanently of rot and standing water. She had arrived to a washed-out winter sky, had found the wetlands gray instead of green, the cypress trees standing bare and skeletal in stagnant water. The air still carried humidity, but winter had stripped the bayou of any softness. Everything looked exposed. Dead grass flattened beneath cold wind. Thin fog hanging low over the marshes. Long empty roads disappearing into drowned forest. Absolutely depressing.

She had spent most of the flight and the subsequent week reviewing the files her contact had given her. It had been much, just unconnected fragments, however. Some insurance claims, incomplete autopsy reports, investigation debriefs from different authorities. Several federal requests originating from different agencies had been submitted regarding the same geographical region. Different chains of command asking for identical information. It looked like some requests had been approved and others denied, but for no comprehensible reasons. More like on a random basis. It was weird. One file carried a BSAA reference number. Another contained the same request under a different classification and from a different agency. The headers and signatures were different but the body wording was not. It was strange. It made it look like multiple groups inside the same institution were searching for something without wanting the respective others to know.

The deeper she dug, the more untransparent it became. A BSAA logistics report referenced surveillance equipment deployed to South America. But no corresponding operation existed. Personnel transportation records showed flights that had officially never been carried out. Equipment had been requisitioned. Fuel consumed. Teams moved. Yet no mission reports had followed. The paper trail simply stopped. Someone had buried it. Or it had, in fact, never existed. Neither explanation seemed satisfying. She thought about what her contact had said. Off-grid deployments. It now looked increasingly supposable. The question was why.

A few years ago she would have suspected Umbrella behind it immediately. Or Simmons, or his family. They had built entire careers around compartmentalization and manipulation. Information had been their preferred weapon. But Simmons was dead, Umbrella had long fallen. Yet somehow she recognized the same patterns. It somehow annoyed her in a way it rarely had before. After everything that had happened in Raccoon City, in Terragrigia, Lanshiang. Whole organizations still built private empires around bioweapons. And one of them had now perhaps send her to estimate how big their influence still was. She sighed, decided to leave the BSAA be for the moment. She had another lead.

The vessel. The one her contact believed had disappeared in the Gulf. A shipping intermediary probably connected to it had surfaced years earlier during an entirely unrelated investigation. Not under that name. Under another. A shell company which’s ownership trail dissolved quickly as she looked deeper into it. What struck her though was it’s legal representative. Just a law firm with three separate lawyers that seemed to do nothing big. But in this context, she found it too low key to not be noticed. No visible leadership. No ideology. Just infrastructure. Money. Transportation. Acquisitions. The kind of network designed to move things without attracting attention. She tried to connect the names to Umbrella, to Tricell. But there was nothing. Just nothing. Wesker had loved control. Simmons had loved power. But this felt different. Like sand running through her fingers.

Or perhaps like people who had watched both men fail and learned from it. No monuments. No speeches. Only quiet expansion. As she typed the names of the lawyers into several search engines and let them run, she strangely stumbled over another BSAA internal memo. Most of it had been redacted. But one line had remained. Potential foreign non-state actor involvement cannot be excluded. It was bureaucratic language written by lawyers. But it seemed like somebody inside the BSAA had already arrived at the same conclusion she had. Someone at least expected another player existed. But apparently, nobody had been willing to look into it deeper. Which was odd. Or was it? Perhaps someone had struck his nose into this while it hadn’t been his business and while he lacked the required authority. Which brought her thoughts back to Chris Redfield. For the first time she understood why her client had mentioned him specifically. Not because Redfield was compromised. But perhaps the opposite. If Redfield had discovered the same discrepancies like she just had, she expected that he would have kept digging. And perhaps eventually find something out. Which made him dangerous. To her client. To the people funding the operation. But whatever he was or was not getting at currently, Redfield was not the threat. He was another investigator probably but not necessarily approaching the same door from a different direction. And judging by the missing reports, someone was working very hard to make sure that said door, whatever it was, remained closed.

She sat in the dim light of her motel room, one foot resting on the edge of the chair opposite her. The laptop screen cast pale reflections across the room while rain tapped softly against the window. She felt tired. But she knew her sources were exhausted at this point. She had to make a move into the field. Well. The question was where to begin. The missing people. The vessel. The BSAA. Three trails, all potentially connected and potentially not. She studied the notes she had accumulated over the past weeks. The disappearances were the most obvious starting point. Victims left families, friends, employers, witnesses, paper trails. She could spend the next several days retracing every movement of every person who had vanished. And she was sure it would produce results. The problem was that she would be approaching the situation from the very end of the chain. Whatever had happened to those people had already happened. There was no urgency about it. She could pursue it at any given time now.

Then there was the BSAA. The internal contradictions bothered her. But it wasn’t really her mission to look into it. Chris had to handle his stuff by himself, even though everything looked hard like a power struggle or compartmentalization. Perhaps both. Still even if she uncovered what Chris Redfield and his people were looking for, she would still only be studying another investigation. Following investigators instead of following the thing they were investigating rarely lead to uncompromised or even different results. No, it wasn’t an option.

Which left her with the vessel. The one thing that had existed before all the others. Before the disappearances. Before the strange medical reports. Before the BSAA’s interest. Before somebody had started burying paperwork. She stared at a map displayed on her screen. The vessel occupied a unique position. It sat at the beginning of every timeline she had assembled. Remove it and the rest became disconnected incidents. It was her starting point. And even a promising one, because ships required infrastructure, crews, ports. People. Someone had known what was aboard. Someone had expected it to arrive and noticed when it hadn’t. That meant the vessel offered something the disappearances did not. It offered a chance to move upstream toward the source. People left traces. She was proven right when she found something about four hours of research later. There was a name from a former coast guard. He had meanwhile retired, ran a small work shop for small ships these days. Officially he spent his retirement there, repairing boat engines near Morgan City. But a few calls with some of her informants later she found out that unofficially he had worked several salvage operations in the Gulf after leaving service. One of them had occurred suspiciously close to the date of the vessel's disappearance. It was enough for her. She decided she would go to see him the next day.

She liked the smell of the old workshop which sat at the edge of a narrow canal. It was metallic, rusty perhaps with a hint of oil and gasoline. She inhaled. It had something clean, something pleasantly bare of anything humane. For as long as she could remember, she had strongly disapproved of any kind of body odor. Odors were more present than pictures. They couldn’t be avoided. When you didn’t wish to see, you closed your eyes. When you didn’t wish to feel, you gritted your teeth. But a scent… It was pervasive. Because you needed to breathe. And if you tried to evade it by not letting it reach your nose, you would taste it on your tongue. Inescapable, almost impossible to forget once experienced. It had been the one thing she had never been able to escape from. And she hated it. With only one singular exception. She bit her lip. No. No thoughts in this direction.

She let her fingertip drive over an old engine as she walked deeper into the hall. There were discarded propellers, fishing equipment. The entire property looked one strong storm away from collapsing into the water. Endearing, in a strange way. A second later she realized that the owner himself looked no better. He stood behind poorly lit counter, seemed to be working some parts that looked like those of an outboard. His beard was gray, his skin permanently tanned from sun damage. His eyes looked clear though, focused. As she approached, his gaze snapped up. "Sorry Lady, shop's closed" he informed. She halted. “I don't need repairs." His brows furrowed into a frown as he regarded her. "Yeah. Makes sense. Did you get lost?" “Not exactly.” He fully straightened now, crossed his arms before his chest, still holding on to his screwdriver. “Then what do you want?”

She smiled. “Straight to business, I like that attitude. It saves time.” “Like I said. I have nothing to offer for you.” She nodded slowly as she started walking again until she stood right before his work bench. So a moment she played with the parts before her, turning them in her fingers. “Why not let me decide after our conversation is over?” The old man wiped grease from his hands with a stained rag. “Whatever it is you desire to talk about. I am not interested.” She arched a brow, lazily drew her gun from its holster to check the magazine, clipped it back, unlocked the safety, looked back at him, without raising the weapon just yet. “Does this change your mind on this, by any chance?” She watched him for a second before she reached into her jacket and threw ten thousand dollars on the table. “Or that?” His eyes drifted briefly toward the rolled up notes. It wasn’t enough money to impress him. But enough to make him curious. “Information costs more than repairs." She turned her gun, metal reflecting in the fading light. "I was hoping you’d give me a discount." He huffed. "Depends what you're asking." She reached again to slide a photograph of the vessel across the bench which she had found on the drive she had received, watched his reaction to it intently. It was neutral. Unnaturally so. There wasn’t even the faintest spark of interest. He had recognized it and suppressed it immediately. Bingo.

"You know it." He locked gazes with her. "No." She stared back. “Don’t be under the illusion you could fool me. I know people like you. I have dealt with them for all my life. And I can only suggest you spare yourself any unnecessary measures from my side to bring you to talk. Because I will. And then we get to the really expensive part.” "I hate people like you." She smiled. “Why spend more time with me than necessary then?” That earned a reluctant snort. For several seconds only rain filled the silence. Then he picked up the photograph at last. "I never boarded her." She raised her brows. “But?" "But I heard about her." She simply waited. Silence was often more productive than questions. Eventually the old man spoke again. "They said she wasn't carrying what the paperwork claimed." "Which was?" "Medical equipment." She wasn't surprised. "Do you happen to know what she was actually carrying?" The old man scratched his beard. "No. We weren't able to recover the cargo back then. Only a few items.” She surveyed him, didn’t think it was a lie. “Theories?” At that his eyes lifted toward hers. For the first time genuine discomfort appeared there. “Someone like you shouldn’t be digging in things like these.” She narrowed her eyes a fraction. “That is not an answer to my question.” He sighed in unease. "It seemed like it was something alive." She furrowed her brows, didn’t speak. “Animals" he clarified. She had not expected that. “What?” "So they said." “They?” He shifted his weight. “Dockworkers. I haven’t seen them in a long time.” She nodded slowly. "Large animals?" "From the things we were able to retrieve it didn’t look like it would have been suited for the transportation and containment of large animals. But who knows. A lot was lost." There was a pause. Her mind was racing to catch up. The wording he used caught her attention. He hadn’t just said transported or shipped. He had also said contained. “For avoidance of doubt. Was containment the wording those dockworkers used?” He shook his head. “Is it important?” "I’ll tell you after I hear it." He looked toward the canal. Toward the gray water beyond. When he finally answered his voice had lowered. "There were people asking questions after the ship disappeared." She held her breath. “Government plants?" "Some." "Others?" His jaw tightened. "Those were the ones that worried me." She didn’t miss a beat now anymore. "Why?" He looked her deeply in the eye. "Because they already knew the answers. They weren't investigating. They were looking for something they had lost." His fingers tightened around the photograph. She remained silent. So somebody believed they couldn’t afford losing the vessel. “Do you have names or an indication who they worked for?” He shook his head. “Hardly. But…” “What?” she urged now and he hesitated. Then he turned around to take out an old leathery notebook. He started to flip through the pages and handed it across when he was done. It was a rough sketch. Not particularly good but enough. She studied it. Male in their forties. Sharp features, expensive haircut. Generally nothing remarkable. Except she had seen him before. Not the man himself. But a photograph. It was one of the three lawyers.

The old man seemed to notice the change in her eyes. "Know him?" "Maybe." He nodded slowly. “He wasn’t the kind of person one would like to mingle with. If I were you, I'd stop digging." She took out her burner phone and held it up. “May I?” she asked but he simply tore the page out and gave it to her. “You can have it. It is too risky for me anyway. I just thought… Well. I believed the police would be interested someday. But no one ever came and asked.” She folded the sketch and slipped it into her pocket. Made sense. Because their superiors were most likely involved in one way or the other. “Thanks.” He now looked at her a little uncertain. “Can I ask you a question in return?” She kept her expression blank. “Information costs more than repairs” she retorted and therewith made him smile faintly. For a long moment she thought he wouldn't speak again, just keep staring off into the rain. But then he did. “Six months after that ship disappeared, people started vanishing around here. Do you think there is a connection?" For the first time since arriving in Louisiana, she felt the two trails finally touch. The vessel. The disappearances. She was on the right track. She debated with herself what to say. Then she looked at him. “Provided my portrait won’t land in there” she pointed to the notebook “I might share my view on this.” He nodded. And she believed him. He didn’t seem a bad guy after all. “I believe whatever was on that ship, has led to the disappearances. In one way or another. But I am looking to find it out. And also why somebody has gone to such extraordinary lengths to bury the trails. And I will try and make sure it wont cause any more harm in future.” At that she thought that a faint hint of hope touched the man’s eyes.

As she drove away, she discarded the temptation to follow the disappearances next. The missing people were the end of the chain. The vessel was preceding them. If something had indeed arrived aboard that ship, then every victim, every altered medical report and every buried investigation ultimately traced back to it. Chasing the disappearances now would mean studying consequences. She preferred causes. The sketch in her pocket only reinforced that conclusion. Not a scientist. Not security. A lawyer. The head of a shell company. It meant that there had been investors, contracts, ownership structures and contingency plans. Somebody had expected accountability long before things went wrong. The BSAA files suddenly made more sense as well. They hadn’t been investigating random missing persons. They had been looking for the same thing she was. And then stopped entirely. Which was weird. Or made total sense. But she wouldn’t stop. She would find whoever had sent that vessel. And she expected it to be her contractor or one of its affiliates.

By evening she had taken retreat in the motel again into which she had checked about three weeks ago located near the edge of Dulvey Parish, half hidden behind overgrown trees and power lines that buzzed unevenly at night. The owner had barely looked at her when she had paid in advance and in cash. It had been a little odd. As if people here had learned not to ask questions. She had explained regardless, had told the woman she needed somewhere to retreat to after a heavy dispute with her husband and that she appreciated her discretion in case anyone came asking. She didn’t think she had really listened, however. Which had been acceptable as well.

The heater rattled constantly without ever fully warming the room. Pale winter rain streaked down the window beside flickering neon from the motel sign outside. Her coat lay folded over a chair near the door, still damp from canal fog. She had taken a seat on the edge of the bed which was fully covered with spread case files, a disposable cup of terrible coffee cooling untouched in her hand. She looked at her running laptop. She had left it here during her absence. It was currently trying to circumvent a firewall in order to gain access to a system. Chris Redfields personal account, to be precise. She had contacted an asset the other day who had provided her with the necessary intel for an attempt like this. The motel's internet security had resisted approximately forty-three seconds. Redfield's laptop significantly longer. Her system had been running for fourteen hours straight by now. She didn’t assume the reason was that he was so particularly good with computers. But because the BSAA employed people who were. But eventually, the last barrier fell away. At three o’clock in the morning when she had found herself fallen asleep on her files, fully dressed. She struggled up and settled against the headboard, laptop on her knees. "Sorry, Chris" she muttered. Not really. The first thing she looked for was Louisiana. There wasn’t much. Hints about missing people, some intel on the missing vessel. Entirely on the surface, however. Nothing about its cargo, no breakthroughs. No hidden operations, neither secret investigations. Only questions and dead ends. Chris was chasing shadows, even more clueless than she was. Which meant he had nothing she needed. Why was her contact so fucking wary of him then?

The second she looked for was his current notes, the leads he was following. Strangely enough she found though, that they were mostly about Blue Umbrella. Officially, they were what remained after Umbrella's collapse. New management. New funding. New mission statement. They claimed to be cleaning up the mess the original company had left behind and had spent years trying to convince the world they were no longer in the bioweapons business. Some within the BSAA seemed willing to believe that while she had learned long ago that a new logo rarely meant new people. And seemingly so had others at the BSAA. They seemed more skeptical. And Chris appeared to belong firmly to the latter group.

Blue Umbrella representatives had seemingly approached the BSAA repeatedly over the previous years. Offers of cooperation. Intelligence sharing. Technical support. Research assistance. Publicly, it was exactly what everyone claimed. The reformed remnants of Umbrella attempting to atone. From what she took from Chris’ notes, however, things looked considerably less harmonious underneath the smooth corporate surface. Several internal communications questioned their motives. Others defended them. There were arguments, delays, disagreements. Entire chains of discussion ending without conclusions.

She frowned. Chris didn’t seem entirely sure whether Blue Umbrella were partners or a future problem. A recovered report caught her attention. It contained multiple references to bioweapon incidents and one recurring note to himself. Continue monitoring Blue Umbrella activities. Huh. Well. It made her lose interest almost immediately. If Chris had evidence of wrongdoing, she believed he would have acted. But it looked like he had nothing. And really she had no idea why she was looking at it anymore anyway. Blue Umbrella wasn't her lead. And none of her business.

She exhaled quietly. All the work for nothing. Disappointing. But at least she knew now that whatever game was unfolding in Louisiana, Chris Redfield was not ahead of her. Perhaps not even playing along. She was about to disconnect when a folder caught her eye. DONOTOPEN . She stared at it, frowned, a million questions shooting through her head as to why someone would name a file like that. She opened within the split of a second. For someone who claimed to value operational security, the man apparently understood absolutely nothing about human psychology. Then she held her breath. She was looking at a range of photographs. She opened the first one. Chris and a brunette, attractive woman with a physique that impressed even her standing on a beach somewhere. She zoomed in. Jill Valentine. She had never encountered her in person but there wasn’t a doubt. It was taken from a very unfavorable angle. Jill looked annoyed. Chris looked sunburned. She suppressed a sly smile and clicked again, couldn’t help but notice by the file names that the folder had been frequently updated, apparently never sorted through, however. The second one was them standing beside a motorcycle. Another click. Jill asleep in a chair while Chris had apparently taken the picture. She closed that file immediately, randomly skimming over the next preview icons anymore. Despite spending his life fighting monsters and as far as she had assumed being single forever, he certainly owned an impressive number of photographs involving motorcycles and beaches. And Jill Valentine.

She also noticed the directory had a large number of subfolders. For a brief moment she considered continuing. Her interest was undeniably raised. But she stopped herself. This seemed to be a heavy violation of privacy and even she had standards. Sometimes. Or had she? She couldn’t help but wonder regardless. Were they together? Taking from the nature of the pictures she assumed most likely not. At first glance she had seen no foolish selfies, nothing very private. Had he ever even told her? Or what exactly was taking him so long? Or her? Hadn’t she been abducted by Wesker at some point back in the days? And had that kidnapping not set the stage for Chris to eventually take him out at last? She was on the verge of stopping to wonder about intelligence and go looking for reasons they weren’t together instead. Perhaps she would ask Leon when she got back. She deeply exhaled. Leon. She forced herself not to think about what kind of pictures one might find in his personal account. Her gaze flickered to the phone on her nightstand. With no wallpaper of him, no messages. The dark bruises his fingernails had left in her skin had long disappeared. And now there was nothing left of him anymore except the dull ache within her heart whenever she let her guard low enough to allow thoughts of him to infiltrate her consciousness.

She stared at the device for an eternity. She knew his number by heart, he had never changed it since he had given it to her. She also knew he would answer if she called. It did not matter what hour it was. She tore her gaze away, disconnected from the system entirely, closed the laptop and wrapped it up in her arms tightly for warmth, curled on the bed and pulled the blanket over her. It was so clammy here, so uncomfortable. And it had never been more difficult to endure. She had always thought about him, on the reverie. But not like this. Distance had used to feel a lot more effortless. Necessary. She had disappeared, he had stayed behind, and eventually they had moved on. And now the silence felt noticeable. She was catching herself thinking about him automatically throughout the day. Wondering what he was doing, what expression he just wore, what he would think if he were reading these reports. It was absurd how quickly she had adapted to him existing beside her. Or, more accurately, to him invading her space entirely. The space she had fought to maintain personal for so long. She listened to the seemingly never pausing rain slipping slowly thrumming on the motel roof and realized with quiet displeasure that she missed him. Vividly. The warmth of him beside her in bed which the laptop could hardly make up for. The sound of him moving around a room. His ridiculous jokes. His clinginess. His goddamned pasta that even halfway burned tasted better than high cuisine. Fuck.

Her eyes snapped open once more, looking at the phone before she forced herself to turn it face down against the nightstand and squeezed her eyes back shut.

He paused for a moment at the door to make sure she wasn’t on the phone before he knocked against the frame. She turned around and smiled immediately. A real smile that reached her eyes bright and warming. It chased some of the clouds that had haunted him through the day away already. “Hey. What are you up to?” She shrugged. “Nothing big really. I was following up on some leads. You?” “Uhm, I am getting ready for a dispatchment. Brazil. Next week. Just had the briefing. Thus missed your call.” She nodded apprehensively. “Oh. I didn’t know that.” She regarded him for a second longer. “How are things with Ada?” He sighed, shrugged, shook his head slightly, bit back the sharp pain and deep concern that cut him like a knife each time he thought about her recently. He considered brushing her question off, didn’t think he would be successful with it, however. She didn’t look like she would let this go. So ultimately, he decided to simply tell her the facts. “Dunno. Actually, I haven’t seen her in a bit.” Her masquerade slipped at that and she frowned deeply. “What?” She looked like he had just revealed to her that aliens actually existed. Perhaps she would have been even less surprised about that than she was now.

His eyes found the floor, he didn’t really want to discuss it, didn’t know what to say. He heard her move, heard soft steps and a second later she stood before him, picked up his gaze. He reluctantly allowed it. “Are you two no longer… I mean….” she broke off, shook her head, looked entirely lost. He hesitated, wondered what he could reveal to her. That was the thing about letting people in, he thought. He had wanted to come clean about his relationship with Ada and therewith most likely also forced himself to accept that he had exposed it to other people’s judgement.

He looked her straight in the eyes as he replied. “No, Sherry. I am with her. In fact, I don’t think I have ever not been. She just… Isn’t present with me right now.” Her frown merged into something that looked at lot like sadness. “I don’t understand? Has she just left?” He thought about her departure, about her little note that still accompanied him everywhere he went. He had read and re-read it a million times. It’s papery edges had already started to tatter. “She hasn’t just left” he said in a deliberately firm tone, wondered if he himself needed to hear it even more than her. “And when does she come back?” He audibly exhaled. “I don’t know.” She seemed entirely lost, looked so sad that he added “Don’t worry. I was aware of what I was committing to.” Which didn’t seem to lessen her concern. Her expression remained entirely unchanged. After another moment she looked up. “Do you want to have dinner?” she suddenly asked. He really, really didn’t. There was hardly anything he would rather not do right now. But she looked so hopeful at the prospect. It made him wonder if there was something on her own mind as well. Because her expression wasn’t just interest or concern but... The careful kind of faith that came from seeing a straw coming your way while drowning. And she had called him before, hadn’t she? He expected it to have happened for a reason. So, he deeply inhaled, nodded after all. “Lead the way.”

She had walked him to a little restaurant close to the DSO headquarters. He had been there for lunch several times and the kitchen wasn’t bad. They had been offered a nice table by a window and were now regarding the menus in silence. They hadn’t spoken much anymore since leaving the office in general. He expected however for this to change soon. And wondering how he pictured something like this to look like he realized he wanted something that was solidly present, unbreakable, for everyone to see while it gave nothing away. Something that couldn’t be traced back to anything, because neither of them could afford it. But something that was a symbol. A reminder. Something that connected her with him even if it was just in the quietest possible way. And was this really too much to ask for? Well. Probably. Would she see it as a chain? Most likely. Fuck. But it didn’t have to be this way, did it? Because what if it turned out that she would be happy about it instead? If she chose it? God. He stared out across the glistering city lights while the remnants of the dream still crawled unpleasantly beneath his skin and understood with exhausted clarity that he was already far beyond saving where she was concerned.

Chapter End Notes

Here we go with one of my favorite chapters of the entire work. I know it is not singularly focused on them, like usually. But I wanted to look into a plausible way of how both of them would deal with the situation. And I hope you will like it or at least find it comprehensible. Let me know any of your thoughs. I know its massive this time. Apologies for not being able to cut myself short.

Chapter 6: Undertow

At first glance, the missing persons had led nowhere and forty eight hours following her return to Louisiana she had already begun to come to terms with the possibility that none of the victims would provide any hint as to the direction in which she could proceed to find answers about the load of the vessel. What she knew after her conversation with Chase was, however, that her next step had to be exactly that. There was no alternative to it at this point. No other trail to follow. She had resiled from applying more persuasive measures to get something out of the man. And while she resolutely avoided going deeper through her reasons for it, she was still aware of the fact that as a result, her chances of getting anywhere nearer The Connections had shrunk to zero for the time being. Well. At least she now vaguely knew who she dealt with. Still gave her no fucking clue about how to best move on from here.

Perhaps all she could do was look closer. To reevaluate what she had. To look over the facts with the knowledge she had gathered until now. She rechecked. All the files, starting at the very beginning. Paid closer attention. Tried to see what no one else had seen. She had always been exceptionally good at that. And there had to be something. She had talked to the former coast guard who had set her on the lawyer trail. He had mentioned that the disappearances had occurred in a timely connection with the vessel incident. Something was hidden somewhere. All she had to do was find it.

For almost two weeks she moved through Dulvey Parish and the surrounding wetlands, always careful to not being high profile, to not becoming visible or memorable. She changed her motel more often now. She dressed in simple sports attire, put her hair in ponytails, wore no makeup. She spent her mornings in county archives and police stations. Her afternoons on forgotten roads and overgrown hunting trails from where she brought back more insect bites than useful intel. Her evenings inside her motel rooms with maps spread across the bed and her laptop illuminating the darkness.

One by one she plotted and re-plotted every disappearance she could verify. As far as she was able to determine, it were mostly tourists, hunters, fishermen. One drifter. And a local journalist. He had worked for a small local paper in New Orleans. Most of his superficial articles had concerned zoning disputes, environmental issues and corruption investigations.
