Then, several months ago, he had started to write highly speculative things about lights in the wetlands at night, strange sounds and missing animals. Hardly material for a Pulitzer Prize. What all of them had in common, however was the fact that they most likely hadn’t ever met. She followed their trails to different backgrounds, different towns, different years.

Until, one night in the motel while marking maps and highlighting routes, she suddenly noticed a similarity. Every line eventually seemed to point toward the same stretch of countryside. Like rivers feeding the same swamp. She initially assumed it was coincidence. But rechecking all the information she had accumulated so far under this angle, the evidence kept piling up. And so she decided to give it a try. She drove into the region over half flooded roads that deteriorated the farther she came. Asphalt gave way to cracked pavement, then gravel, then little more than a raised strip of mud cutting through the wetlands. The sky hung low overhead, thick with clouds that turned everything grey. Her drive ended before a collapsed bridge blocking the way ahead. She turned off the engine and got out of her car to look around. It was silent here, she noticed. Strangely, there were no sounds of birds. The air smelled of stagnant water, wet earth and decaying vegetation. And something else. A faint sour undertone. Unlike anything else. Repelling. Before her the road disappeared into a sea of green. Cypress trees rose from black water, their trunks thick and ancient. Curtains of Spanish moss hung from branches overhead. Pools of muddy water reflected the dull sky through gaps in the canopy. Everything looked drowned. She crouched near the collapsed section of bridge. The damage wasn't recent. Whoever the owner had seemingly abandoned this route.

As she glanced around though, she spotted faint depressions in the mud where vegetation had been crushed. She went to take a closer look and noticed a broken branch that showed pale wood beneath its bark. It was just a small disturbance. Easy to miss. And still… It looked like someone had perhaps walked here. Into the wetlands. Weird. Perhaps hikers? But at this time of the year? She took out her phone, checked the satellite. There was a property, approximately four miles and a bit deeper into the wetlands. Practically in the middle of nowhere. She zoomed in on the imagery. She saw two roofs, a barn. Outbuildings, perhaps. It looked unmaintained. But not really abandoned. She found what looked like a small access road, scrolled to follow it. It ended… Before that bridge. She squinted. Looked back ahead. Who lived there? A normal property owner would have repaired the bridge years ago. So either there was no longer anyone there or… Someone who preferred isolation. And something else had struck her, yet she didn’t immediately know exactly what. She scrolled back to the buildings. The longer she looked, the more it seemed like some parts of the property appeared darker than the rest. It spread from the main house into the surrounding land and looked like a stain perhaps or… contamination? Definitely not just shadows. Perhaps an oil lack? Or just poor image quality. Anyway. She would have to find out. This didn’t look like nothing. She went back into her car, assumed this would require waterproof equipment, climbing gear, navigation markers. The bushes were thick. It wouldn’t be easy walking through them. Luckily, she’d always had a thing for little outdoor adventures.

He didn’t so much arrive home after an eight hours drive as drag himself into it. The door shut behind him with a dull thud, and he just stood there for a second, keys still in his hand, staring off into nothingness. He shrugged off his jacket, threw it and missed the rack. Outstanding performance. He listened. Heard nothing but silence. “Hello?” he called regardless and, not too unexpectedly, didn’t get an answer. He huffed. Would have been too good to be true anyway. But still it felt nice to be home. He needed a reset. Badly. The week had been hell. And he decided to start by taking a shower.

He went upstairs, undressed and tossed his clothes into the laundry bag. He opened the tab and waited until the shower had run hot enough to emit fog all over the place. Then he stepped inside, let the water prickle on his skin. He placed one hand against the tile, let the heat soak into his muscles that decisively refused to relax. Water ran down his face, over his tired eyes that stung when he occasionally blinked them open. He stayed way too long but when he stepped out, he felt marginally less like he’d been run over. He dried his skin, wrapped the towel around his hips and regarded himself through the mercifully fogged mirror. Still, even though occurred by haze, he looked like trash. And it wasn’t only the week. It was the inability to find sleep that had slowly returned, getting worse day per day. His initial agitation had meanwhile long given way to a permanently underlying concern about her, about what she might be going through.

He tried not to think of it, pulled open the drawer underneath the sink to fetch the blow-dry when his gaze got caught on a small light blue and white package. Neatly placed, like everything she owned. A pair of hylo eye patches. He had often seen it, had never really paid attention to it before though. He took it out to inspect it more closely. Nourishing and soothing. Anti-fatigue. He paused to stare back at his reflection that was starting to clear. Huh. Sounded like it had been custom made for him. He checked the application instructions thoroughly and then carefully took out the patches and placed them underneath his eyes. He adjusted one slightly. They felt pleasantly cool, smelled well, a bit like her. Nice. He went back to the kitchen, hair simply left damp after all, patches in place and felt human enough again to function. He opened the fridge. Inside everything was in order. It was her influence but even during her absence he had done his best to maintain her arrangements. Perhaps because it made him feel less alone. Perhaps because it held a sense of hope. He scanned around lazily, then spotted a sealed glass bottle at the very back. He took it out, turned it in his hand to read the label. Ginger cucumber water. “…sounds like something that fixes your whole life instead of just being something to drink” he muttered to himself. He checked the expiry date then opened it, took a cautious sip. And blinked. Mhh. Another sip. Not bad. Not bad at all.

He went to let himself sink down to the sofa as he slowly emptied the bottle. Things were too quiet. Not just here, not just because she wasn’t there. But in general. Ever since she had left he had painfully carefully watched all developments on the bioweapon sector. And to his surprise, there had barely been any. The DSO hadn’t gotten any leads in this regard. He had picked up something from her about fungus and had gone through every possible source about it. But he had found nothing in open DSO cases.

He flinched when he noticed a sudden, entirely unexpected sound from the off. It took him a fracture of a second to recognize his own ringtone. Someone was calling him. He briefly looked around, disorientated, then remembered that his phone had been in the pocket of his jeans which he had just before discarded into the laundry. He jumped up and started to run so fast that he lost his towel on the way, flew up the stairs until he reached the bathroom and dug out the device. His hand shook as he looked at the screen. He exhaled a shaky breath as he saw the number that was calling. Fuck this. For a second he had thought it might be her. Or perhaps rather hoped. Or not. He wasn’t entirely sure. He cursed under his breath and slid to answer. “Hey there, Kennedy.” He straightened a little. “Hey. Chris.” “Glad to see you are alive” Chris stated flatly after a beat. He chuckled. “More or less. You? Care to make an excuse for ghosting me?” Chris chuckled too. “Sorry man. I was kinda busy.” He let out a deep sigh, muttered “Yeah, I figured.” He started to make his way back downstairs. “Busy as in being in a war zone or about to walk into one?” There was a silence and then a faint exhale audible through the line. Perhaps Chris’s version of acknowledging humor. Damn, these Redfields were so fucking tense these days. It struck him then. Chris did not sound strange at all, did he? Especially not pissed or even concerned. Which was kind of surprising. Initially, when Chris hadn’t called him back he had assumed that perhaps Claire had told him about Ada. That he could be somehow mad at him. Now, however, it rather seemed like he was entirely casual. Because it didn’t matter to him? Or had Claire actually not told him?

“Europe” Chris said shortly and snapped him out of his thoughts. He decided to keep things light for as long as Chris would allow. “I can't recommend it. I've had some pretty bad experiences there with clingy civilians and parasites and corrupt sovereigns.” To his surprise, there was a long pause before Chris answered “…Yeah, no worries. It’s nothing like that.” He narrowed his eyes slightly, the drying patches tugging at his skin. “Okay, that’s not really reassuring. What’s going on?” Chris cleared his throat. “Yeah, well, you know I can’t really hear you. Seems like I have only poor network connectivity.” His brows tensed into a frown. They had once agreed on referring to a poor connection whenever they weren’t able to talk. It set him at immediate unease.

“I see, uhm, okay. Then let’s talk another time perhaps.” And rather sooner than later please. Because my lover who you think you killed years ago has hinted there might be something going on within the BSAA. And because now she is gone and most likely busing herself in the bioterrorism market. All the while I have somehow ended up… staying here. Hydrating. Naked on the phone with damp hair, wearing expensive hylo eye patches, drinking a bottle of cucumber ginger water. He spared Chris that information. “We should definitely catch up. That’s why I’m calling. I wanted to let you know that I will be around DC next weekend. You free?” He collected his towel, squeezed the phone between his shoulder and his jaw while he reattached it. And then dropped back to the sofa, studying the ceiling for a second. “…I am usually not in D.C. around weekends.” He briefly considered offering to make an exception but then discarded the idea. He was already here far too rarely for his own liking. No way. “Oh. Why?” He momentarily hesitated, picked back up the water. “Private reasons.” Now Chris laughed. “Oh damn, Kennedy. Now that clears everything up entirely.” He stared at the bottle in his hand. “I moved to Boston.” In the background on Chris’s end, something distant echoed. Metal, maybe. It sounded like he was somewhere in the open. “Wow, look at you. A townhouse in Beacon Hill too?” He clicked his tongue in annoyance. “Hardly. It’s just… Well. It felt the right thing to do.” “Adding several hundred miles to your commute felt like the right thing to do?” Chris interrogated in an incredulous tone. He audibly exhaled. “Yeah. Turned out I love to be here.” It wasn’t even a lie. “Say what? While the rest of us are living in normal places, apparently Special Agent Kennedy is out there drinking artisan coffee in historic brick neighborhoods." Chris paused, giving him plenty of room to feel awkwardly caught. "Not exactly the first place I'd picture you choosing, though. But makes sense. Who wouldn’t love runs along Charles in the mornings and being surrounded by history and education. I am clearly intrigued. Text me the address. Might come around sometime.” It sounded exactly like the sort of thing a man said before showing up uninvited and staying for weeks. He agreed regardless. “I will.” He heard background noises again. “Sorry Leon, I gotta move. I've learned more about your personal life in thirty seconds than in the previous fifteen years. I need a break.” He couldn’t help but laugh at last. “Yeah, alright. Go take a walk and process it.” Will do. Stay out of trouble, Boston Boy.” “You too, Redfield. Stay alive so you can spend a sophisticated afternoon with me walking along the esplanade, drinking overpriced espresso, and discussing eighteenth-century architecture. You know, experience culture. New concept for you.” He heard a soft laugh that still resonated in his voice when he spoke again. “Later Kennedy.” The line went dead.

He switched on the TV. Mostly to fill the suddenly audible silence. So Claire hadn’t told Chris. She had decided to trust his judgement after all. It warmed his cold insides, filled him with a soft feeling. It broke the heavy clouds over him a little. So much that he felt like calling her. But what would he say? She would ask about Ada. And he didn’t have it in himself to speak about her right now. Touching on the topic with Sherry had nearly cost his composure. He didn’t wish to experience another situation like this any time soon. So he zapped through the channels instead, until he got stuck on the news. He reached for his phone after all and scrolled for a while before he eventually opened his photo gallery. He tapped the first photo of her. There weren’t many that showed her face. But some, nevertheless. All of them unbearably beautiful, so surreal that he briefly wondered if she did in reality look so good because it actually couldn’t be. There was one that tore his heart especially. He had taken it without her knowledge when she had been asleep. He zoomed in. Stared at her serene relaxed features, thumb hovering over her cheeks. For a moment he closed his eyes. Jesus fucking Christ. I miss you so. He remembered that morning vividly. He remembered every moment with her vividly. His eyes traveled to the front door before drifting back to the screen, to her. He kept looking at this picture until his eyes fell closed.

When he woke up on the couch after a restless night, he realized he needed something to do. Anything. Distract himself. Because all he could think about was her. He made himself a quick breakfast and then decided on an outing. He picked back up his leather jacket from the floor, threw it on and started heading to Newbury Street. He would fetch himself one of those artisan coffees Chris had mentioned. Because why not? They were every bit as good as their reputation. Probably not better than the ones his own machine produced. But at least it gave him a destination. The walk took him about ten minutes before he found himself wandering through a street lined with pretty shops, galleries, restaurants and cafés. He let his eyes wander over the beautifully decorated showcases, let his mind drift.

He spent some time in a bookstore, bought some books he thought he might like to read. In a sporting goods store he purchased some overpriced running shoes which at least looked decent, however, and which he planned to try out later. He got himself an annoyingly good, meticulously crafted coffee at a coffee store, was heavily flirted by the waitress, played along until he got annoyed by it and was out again in under three minutes.

A few buildings down he slowed when a boutique across the street caught his attention. Storefront embedded in marble, entrance framed by two cubic windows with dark glass and very discrete displays. Polished metal lettering shone above it and a uniformed doorman stood beside a heavy door. He stared at it blankly. Didn’t move for an eternity. No Kennedy. Please don’t. Just keep walking. His brain also supplied a variety of very reasonable arguments for it. Like the fact that he could barely imagine a moment where he would find himself equipped with enough guts to ask her. Like the fact that even in the unlikely case he did, he still didn’t even remotely know if she would accept it. Like the fact that he was catastrophically getting ahead of himself. Like the fact that he didn’t even know when or even with absolute certainty if she would return. But he kept staring. And, unfortunately, another part of his brain had already betrayed him. The part that remembered her feet in his ankle cuffs, her knee caught in between his, him in her lap in the car, her hands in his hair. Her solid presence through withdrawal. Her lying unconscious in his arms, bullets hammering into his bulletproof vest from behind him. A gun pointed to his head and the entirely out of place feeling of purest relief at the certainty that she was alive and breathing, standing right in front of him. A calculated kiss in a cable car that had felt like butterflies released in his gut regardless. The moment she had blown that van in Raccoon City and the feeling of being struck by a bolt beyond recovery. He was over the street before he realized it.

Inside, the boutique was quiet in the particularly intimidating way expensive places always were. Soft lighting made every surface shine. Thickly carpeted floors swallowed all unpleasant sounds. Glass displays were illuminated like museum exhibits. Everything smelled faintly of polished wood and perfumed air conditioning. He sensed that he should have prepared for this better. Which became obvious approximately four seconds later, when a woman in an immaculate black suit looked at him with the calm attentiveness of someone professionally trained to detect financial instability. And here he stood, unshaved, dressed in t-shirt, leather jacket and jeans, coffee in his hand.

“Good day, Sir.” She smiled very politely, hands folded at her front. “How may I help you today?” He couldn’t bring himself to smile as well, just stared back, a little at loss. About himself mostly. He really hadn’t thought this through sufficiently. But the woman was professional. She just waited, faking and intrigued expression. He cleared his throat. “I was looking for… Rings.” Really narrowing it down there. Her expression remained smooth and elegant though. “Of course. For you? Or for someone else?” He raised his brows. “Oh, uhm. Both, actually.” There was a professional silence but dipped with a small irritated tilt of her head. He really nailed it today. “For a special occasion?” she asked in an attempt to obtain clearer instructions. But the answer to this wasn’t so clear. He wanted to tie the knot with the love of his life. Wanted to make it obvious, permanent. Wanted to commit himself in every way a man could. Preferably receive hers in return. He wanted her to be more than just his girlfriend when for a years he hadn’t even thought that to be possible. He wanted to marry her when he didn’t really think she would. He glanced at the assistant.

“A serious commitment” he answered finally. The associate nodded professionally. “You want to propose to someone?” He inhaled slowly, heavily restrained himself from flinching at the words. Mixed with the way she looked at him, with this composure, it kind of felt embarrassing. “Yeah, but well. Technically not quite. But also…” he grimaced faintly “probably eventually yes?”

The woman’s eyes widened with caution. She looked entirely clueless. He sighed. “I just need two rings. Matching ones.” She blinked. “Oh, I see. But…” she looked a little uncomfortable now. She made eye contact, expression uncertain. “Sir” she restarted carefully “typically one would begin with an engagement ring. Conventionally. Without any intention to interfere… Just...” She trailed off, shook her head. She really must have thought he was the last idiot in the book. “I generally know the process” he reassured her, hoping it would make her feel better. “It’s just that I know I will only have one singular shot for this. And I think if I did the whole kneeling thing” he admitted, needlessly, wondered why he continued at all “she’d either disappear into another identity for six months or shoot me.”

The associate stared at him. “…I sincerely hope you are joking, Sir.” “Fifty-fifty.” To her credit, she only looked mildly alarmed. “She does not wish to marry?” Probably so little that she had never even given it so much as a thought. But perhaps she would consider it if it were something else. A commitment. A vow. No paperwork just… A reminder that told her she wasn’t alone in this world. That somebody was waiting for her to come home. Someone she belonged to.

“Honestly, I don’t think she has ever even considered the possibility. And even if she doesn’t, it would be okay. I don’t need a giant gesture, nothing fancy” he said finally. “I just want something real. Something to give to her to let her know that I am hers. And always will be.” Nice. His heart bared before a stranger shop assistant. Perhaps he needed therapy for a fact. But for the first time, the associate’s expression softened completely. “…You are very certain about her.”

He couldn’t help a chuckle escaping. “Lady” he said, voice coarse “I’ve been certain about her since the Clinton administration.” And at that, she laughed too. Entirely sincere. And something within her attitude changed after that. He could feel it. She did not suddenly become sentimental. But she now followed along. “Well then” she said, smoothing invisible wrinkles from her sleeve. “Let us try to avoid the firearm scenario.” He grinned. “Appreciated.”

She started assembling different models made of different materials and showed them to him. They talked about durability, about grades. And he learned that in terms of endurance, Platinum would be his choice. “It's denser than gold, very robust, naturally white. It doesn't require replating over the years. More expensive, but I think an excellent fit for what you are looking for.” He didn’t care about prices. He needed something strong enough to survive almost anything. And he had to admit that the platinum idea suited him. The word itself sounded solid, permanent and not flashy. Not like shiny gold that immediately drew attention. Which she always did anyway without trying. He received a sample ring he was handed and surveyed it. The metal caught the light rather than demanding it. It was understated, elegant, perhaps a little deceptive, easily mistaken. It reminded him a lot on her. "What are you thinking?" the assistant asked. He realized he'd been staring at the ring for nearly a minute. “Yeah, I think it fits.” The woman smiled and put away the rings she had brough so far, fetched a range of platinum made variants instead. He looked at what she had brought and rejected anything oversized immediately. Anything overly polished. Anything delicate enough to snap. “She works with her hands” he let slip and made the associate pause. “What does your partner do?” He opened his mouth and then closed it again. “Field work” he vaguely offered. The woman accepted that with admirable professionalism. Eventually she pulled out a pair that immediately drew his attention. Simple bands. Elegant. Solid enough to last. Beautiful without trying too hard. Something he held possible she would wear.

The longer he looked the longer he realized he really liked them. They were both entirely alike. No embellishments, classic polish. Uncomplicated on the surface. Indestructible underneath. He picked one up carefully, turning it between his fingers. For its seize it was surprisingly heavy. He pictured it on her delicate finger. Accompanying her wherever she went. He realized the associate watched his expression. She smiled. “I believe” she said quietly “those could be the ones.” He swallowed once. “Yeah”. His eyes locked back on the rings immediately. They were pretty, fit, but… required a little personal touch. Something unmistakable for them, entirely inscrutable to strangers. For a moment he got lost in thought.

“Can you engrave their insides?” he asked. She nodded and retrieved a form block from one of the drawers underneath the counter, together with a pen. “Of course, Sir. Initials? Names?” Names or initials were dangerous leads in their line of work. It had to be something symbolic. She looked at him expectingly. He picked up the larger ring, didn’t hesitate. “A butterfly inside this one.” She looked him deeply in the eyes for a second and he thought that for the first time since he had arrived here, there was a warmth in them. She wrote it down. “And something for the other one as well? “This was the slightly more challenging task. What could be a symbol for his love for her? What symbol would she choose when she thought about him? The associate discreetly left him alone after a minute, saying she was going to get him a glass of water. He was grateful for it, didn’t look at her though. Carefully he took the small ring. What could symbolize how he felt for Ada Wong? For a woman he wanted to be with forever. Who he, through darkness and hopelessness, had never been able to let go. Who had hurt him beyond measure but who had saved his life over and over and not only that but also saved him in every fucking way a man could be saved by another human being. Someone who held him in the palm of her hands, who had locked him in without ever wanting to be let out again. He could only come up with one sensible choice. Handcuffs. One side closed. One side left open. Because he was hers. But she would always be free to leave if she wanted to. He did not intend for her to mistake one for the other. When the assistant returned he told her “Handcuffs. One side left open and one side closed.” Her gaze snapped up. The silence was immediate. The woman looked at him. Then at the ring. Almost apologetic. “Uhm, are you sure… I mean… The symbolization might be misleading… Uhm… Marriage shouldn’t feel like…” He looked at her expression stern. Watched her swallow. “Of course.” She noted it down. When she looked back up, her expression was all wary. She spoke regardless. “Okay. And that would be all?” He shook his head and saw apprehension ghost over her carefully composed expression. “Please add a rocket launcher across from it.” She stared back at him, swallowed hard. “A what?” He wasn’t sure if she wanted him to repeat or if it was rather the fact that she was questioning if he could actually have meant what he had just said. But he decided to keep his answer as neutral as possible. “A rocket launcher. Classic Soviet RPG-7 model.“ Her expression became even more blank. She kept staring at him while he waited for her to start noting it down. She made no move, however, which strongly indicated that his explanation had made it worse. “Sorry. I thought the technical term might help." The saleswoman slowly put down her pen. "Sir." "Yeah?" “Are you really sure about it?” She looked so genuinely concerned now that it made him smile. “Yes. One hundred percent.” And he was. Because nothing defined better what she was for him. The strongest weapon there was. His forever savior. So often a lifeline at the very last minute. In the field and besides. He existed because of her. And she, too, probably always thought of him first whenever she saw a rocket launcher, considering how often she had provided him with one in the face of opponents. The assistant then drew in a deep breath, pushed the form across the counter together with the pen atop of it. “Would mind writing the specification down yourself? I… am not exactly an expert and I wouldn’t want to get it wrong.” Her expression at this was so bewildered that he could but chuckle as he took the pen and started to write. He could almost see her debating whether company policy covered his request at all. “Sure. No problem.” He slid the filled form back to her and regarded her for a second. “You think I am insane.” She sighed heavily, more helpless than anything else. “Oh, uhm… Not necessarily, Sir. I just think I am missing a lot of context.” He huffed, suddenly remembered the compact he’d kept of her since Lanshiang. “Can I send you a picture? For the butterfly?” At that she smiled mechanically. “Certainly.”

About ten minutes later she handed him the dark envelope containing the pick up ticket and her business card with her contact details across the counter. Their gazes locked and for a long moment he was surveyed by her with a certain something he didn’t know how to interpret. Perhaps something like wonder. Or disbelief. Or defeat. “For what it is worth, Sir” she said after a brief hesitation “counter to my initial irritation I don’t think it matters that you won’t take the traditional route to propose.” He smirked. “Thanks for letting me know.” She shook her head lightly, obviously searching for the right words now. “Yeah. Actually, if you allow me to say so, I think… Well. I find it…. Very personal. And I sincerely hope she says yes” she told him as she went to hold the door open for him. He looked down briefly at the receipt in his hand and smiled, arching a brow. “Yeah. Me too.”

She crouched under thick undergrowth at the edge of a tree line and studied the estate through binoculars. The main house sat atop slightly elevated ground overlooking the surrounding wetlands. Once it had probably been beautiful. A large Southern farmhouse with broad porches and white-painted woodwork. Now, however it looked not only abandoned but also degenerate. There were no colors. Just greyscales and mud and clouds obscuring the sky. The air was icy. Moist. Chilling to the bones. And it was entirely quiet. So quiet that she wondered if any life did exist here anymore anyway. Several windows appeared boarded from the inside. One section of the roof looked partially collapsed, covered by a tarp that appeared years old. The surrounding structures were in no better condition. A barn. Workshops. Storage buildings. Rusting machinery infiltrated by vegetation. Nothing moved.

Except the insects humming all around her. They were particularly present here. She slapped her neck and flattened a bug against her skin. Fuck. She hated this place. But there would be no way around it, no way forward other than ahead. She needed to know for sure. So she began her approach. As she trailed towards the compound, she noticed that the path had been recently walked. A few trampled blades of grass and broken twigs gave it away. And when she closed in from the western side of the house she could see smoke rising from the chimney. Someone was here. It gave her an immediate sense of unease. Normal people didn’t live in surroundings like this. Something was deeply wrong with the place. And the closer she came, the stronger became the smell. Like rotten wood. And this sour flavor she had already noticed by that collapsed bridge. Just much more distinct. She passed a rusted pickup truck partially hidden beneath a collapsed shelter. Nearby stood a collection of cages. Dozens of them. Some empty, some filled with animal bones. They looked very old. Nobody had bothered to clean them. A few still contained tufts of fur caught between their bars. She quickly scanned around and when she didn’t see anything, stepped underneath a small porch. Close up, the decay was even more apparent. Mold spread across exterior walls. Water damage marked the foundations. The windows were filthy.

She slipped beneath one and carefully looked inside. A dining room. The furniture appeared antique but not unordinary. Family photographs decorated the walls. They told an entirely different story than the property outside, showed smiling faces and family outings or celebrations. One photograph showed what looked like the father proudly posing beside an enormous alligator. Another showed the family at what appeared to be a county fair. They looked perfectly normal. Happy, even.

She carefully continued around the house. A rear entrance stood partially open. She used a scanner for electronic feedback but there was no signal. No alarm. No security system. No one on watch. How strange. Or not. Perhaps the family that lived here was just a bunch of weirdo’s, dropouts. Something like it. But somehow it made her more cautious rather than less as she squeezed inside, not moving the door an inch. The numbing smell intensified immediately. Mold rotting in stagnant water. How could someone actually live here? But as she looked closer, she found that signs of inhabitation existed everywhere. There were half- emptied food containers. Used dishes. A television sat in one corner, still plugged in despite looking old enough to belong in another decade.

She turned around and her gaze landed on a wall covered with newspaper clippings. She went rigid, froze. What the fuck. Local reports on the missing people. One photograph caught her attention. A reporter. The journalist who had disappeared. The one who’s article had discussed strange disappearances near the swamps. His photograph had been slashed repeatedly with a knife. It spoke a language of outrage. Beside the articles hung several photographs of strangers. Most appeared to have been torn from newspapers or copied from missing-person bulletins. She stared at the collection for a second. Were these people here? Captured? Buried? She averted her eyes, slowly looked around the room again. Somehow this place and the people in it were connected to everything. At least to the disappearances. She just had to find out in which way.

She moved deeper into the house. Where was everybody? She went up a staircase into a dark hallway. The floor creaked constantly underneath her, no matter how trained she was with making no noise at all. If she continued like this, she would soon be discovered, she feared. She drew her gun as she went past closed doors. Halfway down the corridor she froze. At the far end sat an elderly woman in a wheelchair. Motionless, half turned away from her, facing the wall. Her heartbeat accelerated, she feared for the woman to turn her head and see her any moment and her grip tightened around her pistol. She hadn't heard any movement, let alone someone approach. A floorboard suddenly creaked somewhere downstairs and she got distracted by it for a second, glancing in the direction from where it had come. When she looked back to the woman, however, the corridor was empty. No way. She had just seen her. She hadn’t been hallucinating, had she? Or… had she? This smell all about this place. Was it… Poisonous? Shit. She needed to finish this and get out as quickly as she could. She moved further down the hallway when she found one door ajar. Light spilled through the gap. Carefully avoiding to make any sound, she approached. Then she peaked through the narrow opening, held her breath.

It was a room. With a bed, a dresser. A chair beside a window. She listened, still not breathing. Nothing. Slowly she pushed the door open another inch to see better. She was hit by a distinct smell. Unlike the rest of the house. One she was very used to. It smelled like disinfectant. She furrowed her brows, took a step forward until she stood in the doorframe. The room looked less neglected than the rest of the house. Someone was living in here. A child's drawing hung beside the dresser. Crude stick figures holding hands beneath a bright yellow sun. FAMILY had been written across the top in uneven letters. She subconsciously shook her head. How could a room like this exist in a house like the one she was currently in? Her attention fell to a stack of papers atop the dresser. She glanced behind herself once more and then went inside to see. Most were handwritten notes. She tried to read them but the writing was partially hard to decipher and it seemed like it wasn’t of importance. One page caught her eye regardless. It consisted entirely of brief observations, written down in obvious haste.

She keeps asking for a family. Jack keeps calling her sweetheart. Lucas took one of the cameras again. Marguerite says she hears her crying at night. I saw the old woman in the hallway again. Nobody else thinks that's strange.

She keeps asking for a family had been underlined several times until the paper had nearly torn. So whoever had written this had seen the woman in the wheelchair, too? Then she had been real? Shit, but had she seen her before? But it was still so quiet. It didn’t seem highly likely. Whatever. She would simply have to handle it if something occurred. She browsed further through the stacks when she found a portable drive. It was labeled. E-001. For a moment she stared at the wall before her. She couldn’t look into it without a laptop. She debated with herself whether to just take it along but ultimately decided against it. Whoever owned it would notice. And then they would know someone was here. And she had no idea what was going on in this place yet. She couldn’t risk being uncovered. She sighed. She would have to find a computer then. Could become a challenge in a place like this.

She carefully returned the stack of papers exactly as she had found them. The drive went back on top. She checked its orientation twice before stepping away from the dresser and quietly returning into the hallway. The old floor groaned beneath her weight and she froze. Fucking shit. The sound seemed absurdly loud in the silence that surrounded her. But nothing happened and so she continued slowly. The level was larger than she had initially assumed. There were at least five doors. All of them closed. Opening would be a risk. But the floor seemed abandoned. Water damage had eaten through ceilings and wallpaper. Black stains spread across entire sections of wall. It looked like a plant, maybe. She stepped close to inspect it, switched on her flash light at it’s lowest setting to not raise any potential attention. The growth wasn't flat. It protruded from the wallpaper in dense clusters of black fibers, twisting and interweaving into one another. Thin strands disappeared into cracks in the woodwork and reemerged several feet away, creating a network that seemed to spread through the entire structure. Like roots. Or veins. The surface glistened, pulsated, reflected light almost organically. She looked closer, breathed through her mouth to avoid the rotten smell, tasted the earthy taste on her tongue regardless. She took a knife from her pocket and scraped a small section away. The black mass peeled from the wall with surprising resistance. Long fibrous strands stretched before they separated.

She examined the sample. It wasn't a plant. It rather looked like muscle tissue. She brought the sample closer to the flashlight, smelled after all. It smelled like the rest of the air. Decay in its purest form. She realized that the fibers formed branching patterns beneath the surface. Thousands of microscopic threads woven together into larger structures. She looked back at the wall, eyes following the lines now. It looked like it grew toward something. Or had grown from something. It appeared like the house was suffering from an infestation and for a brief moment she found herself wondering how much of it existed beneath the estate. How deep it went. Then her flashlight caught something. She leaned closer than she felt comfortable with and looked. Embedded within the mass was a white shape. Small. She brushed away some of the surrounding fibers and uncovered a bone. Telling by its size one of an animal most likely. The black strands wrapped around it so completely that they had begun growing through tiny fractures in the surface. Incorporating it. It made her stomach turn. Whatever this was, it wasn't merely growing on organic matter. It was feeding on it.

This was not ordinary. Perhaps this was what her client had been looking for. But looking at it here, it hardly was just a fungus, hardly just a parasite. She had never seen anything like it. She remembered what her client had told her. Integration on a cellular level. She reached into her pocket and took out a small containment bag. She placed the sample inside and carefully put it away. Then she turned around to continue her search for a laptop. She needed to know what was on that stick. She was in the right place. Now all she had to do is find out what was going on and leave.

Every now and then she encountered signs of recent use. Recently extinguished lamps. Blurred dust on surfaces. One doorway stood partially open. Inside she briefly caught sight of children's toys stacked on shelves beside several VHS tapes. None of it seemed to belong in a house like this. Who was it that lived here? The family, she had seen on the pictures in the dining room maybe? The thought made her increasingly uncomfortable. She reached the far end of the hallway. A staircase descended into darkness. The moment she approached it she heard voices. She immediately took a step back and listened. Male and female. They seemed to be downstairs. She crouched beside the railing and carefully looked through the gaps. Nothing. Ever so carefully, she descended the stairs. And suddenly saw them. Fuck. She flew back up then peaked below. Two people had reached the middle of the hallway, not far from the stairs. An older woman and a man probably the same age. Both worn but apart from that pretty normal. Not scientists or mercenaries or anything. Neither of them looked remotely threatening. Also, the way they spoke was rather… Casual. She held it possible that they were the same that had been in the pictures. She remained completely still.

“She ate?" the man asked and the woman nodded. "A little." "Good." The conversation sounded ordinary. Like listening to neighbors discussing family matters. "Is she still hallucinating?" The man sighed. “No. I don’t think so. Poor thing.” She frowned. Poor thing? That wasn't the reaction she had expected. It sounded like genuine concern and not like some terrorists experimenting on B.O.W.’s. "Maybe she'll get better eventually" the woman said quietly. "Of course she will. We are here for her. Families’ supposed to stay together. Ain't that right, sweetheart?" The words lingered. Something about the way he said them. Jack keeps calling her sweetheart. So was this Jack?

A sudden crash somewhere deeper inside the house made her tense. A second later came a burst of laughter. Male. Young. Followed by what sounded suspiciously like breaking glass. She immediately heard Jack close in on her and for a split second she froze entirely, fearing he would notice her. But then his footsteps descended deeper into the hallway. "Lucas! You stay outta that workshop unless you want another whipping!" His voice boomed through the house. No answer. The woman sighed heavily. "Boy's probably breaking something again. Let’s check.” She heard them walking, floors cracking underneath their footsteps and she saw her opportunity.

As the couple disappeared from view she carefully went down, withdrew from the staircase and continued down another hallway. She had to avoid windows from now on, she reminded herself. What the voices had revealed came in handy, however. There were at least three people present. Jack, Lucas and that woman. Perhaps the woman in the wheelchair. And most likely one more. The woman who occupied the room upstairs and who suffered from disorientation. Three of them were somewhere downstairs together with her. The potential other two women anywhere on the property. The chances of remaining unnoticed were shrinking rapidly. A doorway ahead caught her attention because, unlike the others, it stood slightly open. She went to look inside. It was empty. She tiptoed forward, turned on her flashlight. It was an office. She involuntarily smiled from relief, let her eyes wander over filing cabinets and shelves. And then she spotted it atop a desk. A laptop. It looked relatively modern compared to everything else in the estate. It’s power cable was attached, it had recently been used. She took it, was halfway across the room when she heard footsteps. Close. Very close.

Her heart dropped. Someone was approaching. Fast. She looked around, quickly pressed herself beside a cabinet. The footsteps entered the hallway, passed by the office and… Stopped. She held her breath. Someone was standing right before that door. Her heart hammered in her chest and she feared it would audible. For a long moment there was no movement and she stared off into the darkness, ready to be attacked any second.

Then a young male voice sounded from somewhere beyond the door. "Mom?" Perhaps this Lucas. No response came. And the footsteps resumed and faded away. She waited another ten seconds before breathing again. Only then did she allow herself a slow exhale. Goddamn.

She went to the door and listened. But there was nothing. And so she slipped back into the hall to begin her journey back upstairs. At one point she heard the male’s voice somewhere and immediately ducked into an adjacent room until he passed. Having reached the first floor she caught movement through a window and nearly drew her weapon before realizing it was only tree branches shifting in the wind. Shit. But eventually, she reached the room with the stick and quietly shut the door behind her.

She found that the laptop wasn’t even password protected. Weird as hell. But well. In an area like this, perhaps data security didn't rank particularly high among people's concerns. The drive had remained where she had found it. She connected it at last and let herself down on the edge of the bed. For a second nothing happened. Then a directory opened. The contents were far more organized than she had expected, certainly not those of somebody's personal collection of files. There were folders containing reports, memoranda, research results. Exactly what she had been looking for. Now tell me everything about that vessel. She looked for ‘transportation’, several files popped up as a result and she came across some briefing memos. She held her breath. She opened the first one. It was about routes, about Mexico as a research site. The destination, perhaps where the ship should have ended. She clicked the next one. Browsed. Two agents had been dispatched to observe a ship transporting a cargo to a facility in central America. Mia Winters and Alan Droney. She minimized the file and typed the names, learned that Alan had been killed on the ship. Mia, however had survived the shipwreck. Ironically enough she also learned that the ship had fallen off course as a result of a weather incident. Huh. How extremely unlucky. She checked for Mia Winters, found a personnel resume. A photograph grazed its first page. A middle-aged woman, with dark hair and brown eyes. Young. Unremarkable. She gathered that Mia had been a researcher for a company she had never heard of. F Foundation. For a moment she hesitated. She would have to call Chase and ask about it, she decided. Perhaps he had heard of it. Or them. Mia Winters or Alan Droney.

She stilled for a second, listened behind her. Still no sound. She glanced towards the window. It would be getting dark soon. She quickly turned down the brightness of the screen and reverted to the agent’s briefing. Scrolled, looking for cues as to what had been the cargo, casually picked up that seemingly only these two agents had been present. No security apart from that. She found a reference to a research file, typed its classification into the search window. And found a hit. She clicked.

It elaborated on an individual. Serial number E-001. An attached photo showed a little girl. Perhaps ten years old or so with dark hair and utterly pale skin. She bit her lip. Something inside her moved at the sight, it hurt to see. A second image followed. The same child seated in a wheelchair. The accompanying medical notes referenced accelerated aging and severe cellular degeneration. She frowned. The face had remained unmistakably that of a child. Only the body appeared old. And that wheelchair… Who had she seen in the hallway? Or rather… What? It made her stomach twist. But she forced herself to remain focused. Whatever she felt didn’t change the facts. She learned that the girl, the subject had been engineered, had been the result of a variation of tests and modifications of preceding specimen. What the report didn’t elaborate on, however, was for what reasons she had been created. As a bioweapon? Or something else?

Suddenly, something else caught her eye. The subject was apparently capable of producing an unusual fungal organism. The report described it as a biological network. A carrier. An extension of the host. Was that what she had just seen? That black growth that spread through the walls and the structure of the house? One attached image showed a tissue sample completely overtaken by black fibers. Another displayed a microscopic scan. The resemblance to the growth was unmistakable. It wasn't contamination. It wasn't mold in the traditional sense. It was part of the subject itself. Well, then fuck. She had cut right into it. And another realization started to form in her mind. Her client had already suspected something like a fungus. His data had clued her up on it in the first place. How had they known? They had referred to something like black market rumors, handlers… But looking at the case now it seemed vastly inconceivable. She had seen the family, the house. This wasn’t a professional facility. This didn’t look like the intention to make business. This looked like… A habitat. And that meant that her client most likely had known of the reports. Because they had been the one’s who had created E-001? And lost it during the shipwreck and had now send her to retrieve what had been lost? To see if it was still out there somewhere?

She now remembered other things too that didn’t add up. The reports she had seen that had dated back years. This couldn’t have happened here. The people weren’t that old. Also, the vessel had only washed ashore somewhere around three years ago. So what was the source? And where? The reports indicated nothing in this regard.

She browsed over a few laboratory images. Copies of microscopic scans showing dense networks of dark fibers spreading through tissue samples. The resemblance was unmistakable. It meant that whatever grew inside this house had originated with the child. The subject was here. And she had just cut through the tissue with a knife. She slowly looked up from the screen. The walls seemed narrower than before as she became acutely aware that the growth wasn't limited to a room. It was in the walls. The floors. The foundations. Potentially the entire structure. She had to get out of here as soon as she could. But not just yet.

She forced her attention back to the laptop. Forced herself to breathe. Just finish assessing what is going on, then fuck the hell off. She had a sample. She would have context. And the rest her client would have to handle alone. Like the fact that she had no clue what had happened to the missing civilians and in which way they played a role in all this.

After some more browsing, she found a file set of notes. They weren’t formatted, didn’t follow any style guide. For the most part, they didn’t even consist of full sentences. It looked as if someone had written them personally for themselves. Like journal entries. They mentioned that the tanker had suffered an accident as a result of a storm. That the child had escaped containment. And killed Alan. It were Mia Winters’ notes.

The next entry stated that the child and her had ended up in Louisiana, that a family, the Bakers had taken them in. Soon, however, Mia seemed to have observed behavioral changes in the people. Qualities that hadn’t been displayed before. Also, it was mentioned that Mia had begun finding traces of the fungal organism within the walls of the house. Her notes seemed to have become frantic as time went on. Her writing became less analytical but started to carry an undertone. One of angst. Of desperation. For a moment she wondered. Where was this Mia now? Was she still here? Where exactly was the subject? Why had she seen this woman in the wheelchair? She really really needed to hurry up.

The last entry she looked at wasn’t long. Only a few sentences. But she found herself staring at it forever. First of all, the Mia seemed to believe the Bakers had become infected. THE BAKERS ARE INFECTED was written in bold letters. So could this spread? How? The air suddenly felt much harder to breathe. But she puller herself together to not fall into a panic now. She felt alright. She hadn’t had any contact with the substance. She was wearing gloves. She had no open wounds. And yet she felt deeply uneasy. Fragments of her research infiltrated her mind. Strains, regenerative properties, strange behavior, hostility, effects on consciousness, integration on a cellular level.. She inhaled. The other sentences at the bottom of the page were the same phrase, written over and over again. I need to call Ethan. I need to call Ethan. I need to call Ethan. I need to call Ethan.. Who the hell was Ethan?

She quickly searched the browser for the name and found two videos of who she recognized from the personnel file as Mia, most likely captured by herself. She watched without tone. One was shot on sea, weather beautiful, blue ocean in the background. Mia seemingly bright. The next one, however, was a bit disturbing. Mia seemed frantic, cried. The sequence ended abruptly. She would have wanted to hear what she said exactly but she couldn’t risk it now. Regardless, she could read enough from her lips to understand a few words. Stay away. Don't come. But she decided it didn’t matter. Whoever Ethan was, he wasn’t her problem. She took some pictures of the laboratory details, some of parts of the reports and safely tucked her phone away before she closed the laptop. She needed to put it back to it’s original place. She drew in a deep breath. Okay. Let’s get moving. It had meanwhile turned fully dark outside.

She carefully rearranged the files and the stick on the desk and picked up the laptop. She would bring it back now. She crossed the room and for a moment listened. There was nothing but utter suppressing silence. And silence was always dangerous because it gave nothing away. But she had no choice. She couldn’t stay here forever and so she very gently eased the door back open. The hallway seemed empty. No wheelchair. No elderly woman. Nothing. Perhaps she had just imagined it after all. But it was a strange coincidence. No, it hadn’t been. She was certain that she had seen someone there.

The office downstairs wasn’t far but she couldn’t move fast. Agonizingly slowly she crept over faintly cracking wood when all instinct told her to make a run for it. But that wasn’t her way of operating. Her objective was to remain undiscovered, to not raise attention. And if she broke her orders in this regard, she would be as good as dead anyway. She stilled for a second as the sound of wind against the windows startled her. Fuck. She continued, down the stairs until she could already see the office door. It was still only ajar. Exactly like she had left it. No one had been there. No one had realized she had borrowed the device she was still clinging onto. She closed the remaining distance, squeezed inside and put the laptop back. She adjusted it’s angle, plugged it back in. No one would notice. Unfortunately, the windows here were barricaded. Would have been very smooth. But she wasn’t that lucky.

Her heartrate slowed regardless as she went to leave the room, already thinking of potential exits. But then she heard voices. So suddenly, so unexpectedly and so close that she froze in place, for a second turning entirely immobile. "...thought I heard something” the male voice said. Jack’s. Oh shit. Another voice answered. Male as well, younger though. Perhaps this Lucas but she couldn’t understand what he said. The words were impossible to make out. But they had come from the same hallway. And then there were footsteps approaching. She moved instantly while at the same time mapping out what she had seen of the house. The office offered nowhere to hide. There was no closet, no suited space beneath the desk. The other doors she had not opened. The voices grew louder. Fuck. She had to move, or else she would be trapped in here.

She took out her gun, unclipped the safety, slipped out of the room. To her luck, the hallway was still empty. The staircase lay behind her. However, in the same direction as the footsteps that grew more distinct with every second she hesitated. Fuck fuck fuck. Think. Think. Think. She couldn’t really. All she knew was that going down wasn’t an option. Floor wise but also personally for her. And so she moved as fast as she could to reach the staircase before they would.

She managed, stepped up. A door opened somewhere below. Perhaps the office. Jack’s voice echoed upward. "Lucas?" The answer came immediately. And much closer than she expected. "Yeah?" Her stomach dropped. Oh shit, he was here too. He was upstairs. She had just run directly toward him. And below was Jack now.

A figure emerged from out of the shadows at the far end of the hallway and for the split of a second, she found herself taking him in. He looked young and thin somehow. Something metallic hung from his belt. Wire. Components. The sort of junk somebody collected when they enjoyed taking things apart. He kind of looked sick. Then she forced herself to move. As quickly as she could she turned, opened the first door before her, knowing full well she had no idea what would wait for her behind it and entered. She carefully closed it, avoided making any sound and forced herself to not breathe in the darkness. She expected anything from glowing eyes appearing to the door being ripped back open and Lucas finding her. But nothing happened. The darkness swallowed her and the footsteps moved over until they descended down the stairs.

She counted the seconds, then slipped her flashlight on, not very brightly, however. It looked like an abandoned bedroom. The sheets were dirty. Apart from the bed though, there was no furniture. From downstairs she heard that they were still sweeping the house. She needed somewhere to hide. And then they came closer again. “Have you checked upstairs?” the male asked. “No. I just came from my room when you called.” “Is Mia there?” There was no answer but she heard them approach. Fuck.

She darted out of the room again, evaluated her options. She needed a place to hide. The last room down the hall was Lucas’ room, she couldn’t go there. She also couldn’t go to Mia’s room. There hadn’t been anywhere to hide in there. She decided to try the room next door to it, the one into which she had been able to peak before. There were no alternatives anyway as she heard the staircase squeak. She moved fast and slipped inside just in time before they arrived on top of the stairs. Outside chaos erupted. Heavy footsteps closed in. But they didn’t speak anymore which she would have preferred. She had no idea what they were planning.

She briefly put on the flashlight to see. At first glance it seemed to be some kind of waiting area. Which was strange. Children's books occupied one shelf. Several old VHS tapes another. A faded drawing hung crookedly on the wall. A family holding hands beneath a bright yellow sun. Hadn’t she seen it before? In Mia’s room? But she couldn’t think about it for too long. “Let’s check the rooms, just to be sure.” Okay fuck. Her eyes scanned the room desperately. But there was just nothing. Just small chairs and low tables and some shelves and dried pot plants. Damn. But then she noticed the collapsed section of wall beside the wardrobe. A dark cavity hidden behind broken plaster. It looked as though something had crashed through the wall years ago and nobody had ever repaired the damage. Painfully small and for a moment unbidden memories from her past flooded her brain and her heartrate picked up. It would have to do.

The footsteps reached the door. And she squeezed into the opening. Wood scraped against her shoulders, dust filled her lungs as the space swallowed her whole. A second later the door opened. Somebody entered. They didn’t turn on the light however, and she could see nothing. She heard breathing. Slow. Human. Far too close. And then she was hit by something hard and wondered what was happening and the next moment there was…. Nothing.

Chapter End Notes

Given that this is already my second RE work, I wanted to at least try and give off some genuine Resident Evil vibes this time around. Let me know how it went for you (if you'd like). Also, I hope you'll enjoy the little side events as much as I enjoyed writing them.

Chapter 7: Convergence

Darkness surrounded her completely. Like thick clouds all about her. Like a dream, close to waking up. She knew she wasn’t in the right place, just couldn’t snap out of it. She couldn’t hear through the muteness, tried to open her eyes. But each time she did all she saw was blackness pulling her under once again.

The next time her consciousness flickered was when she heard a sound. Distant, muffled. But something nevertheless. Or was it just waves crushing? Or blood rushing in her ears? But no, not quite. And…. Oh. There was pain. Sudden and sharp. Hammering in her skull. She tried to push through the haze, tried to ignore the ache. Because if she managed to get to the surface, it perhaps meant there would be options. But she couldn’t dwell on it, because suddenly she felt a pang of alertness, harsh and crushing. Leon. Where was he? Was he safe? Was he injured? Did he need her? She breathed in, perhaps searching for the solace of his smell, his warmth. But there was just rotten mire. She needed to get through to him. Get this together. Where the hell was she?

She opened her eyes, saw just blur, blinked several times, cursed herself for not being able to adapt faster. But she felt so heavy, her body was unresponsive. Adrenaline pumped into her veins, her heart started to race, she was in danger because something was there. Movement. Someone. Her vision started to clear as she wiggled to straighten. Which wasn’t an option. She glanced around, memory slowly returning. She was still in that hole in the wall, she realized. In her subconsciousness the fear of Leon being exposed still lingered. But she seized back control over her mind bit by bit. She brought a hand to her head. It hurt like hell and a second later she realized where it had come from. Apparently, as she had retreated here, she had been hit by a brick. It was now resting heavily on her shoulder and she would have liked to shrug it off but she forced herself to remain still. She mustn’t make a sound. But, as it seemed, that damn thing had been the reason for her passing out. Great luck. But the hell with it. She was alive.

She peaked through the small gap in the wall. The woman she had seen with Jack before, maybe Marguerite, was sitting on one of the chairs, knitting. It meant she couldn’t get out. She would have to wait. Everything burned, every bone, every articulation, all of her skin and her throat. She felt terribly thirsty. But she had no choice. She needed to wait it out.

A bit later she realized that the room seemed to be far more used than it had initially looked like. Jack Baker came twice. She couldn’t tell how long he stayed each time. But long enough to remain present as she faded in an out of consciousness. She hadn’t realized it at first but she noticed by the change of illumination about the room that she kept drifting. The boundaries between minutes and hours blurred until she no longer knew how much time had passed. Most likely she had a concussion or something. But whatever the verdict wouldn’t help her now. She needed to make it out. And yet she remained folded inside the narrow hollow behind the wall, muscles gradually stiffening until every joint protested. Her shoulders screamed from holding the same position. It added to the hammering pain in her skull. Her eyes fluttered shut.

As she indistinctly registered footsteps she heard the thundering voice of her father around the house. Her mother crying, screaming. She felt tremors in her body she couldn’t quite put down to a particular reason before she realized that she was trembling. Go away. Please go away. Moisture ran down her cheeks but she didn’t sob, didn’t move. She was too afraid to even breathe properly. Hide. Be very quiet. They must not hear. Don’t look, keep your eyes closed. When you see him he sees you. And he must not see.

At some point she became aware of another voice. In the distance. It wasn’t her mother’s voice. She had never heard it before. She listened. It came from the other side of the wall. It caused her eyes to shoot open as her heart skipped a beat. She swallowed dryly. She was still in that damn hole. She wasn’t a child. And that alone was enough relief to bring her fully back to presence. Everything was better than that. Rather die in here than ever living through these moments again. She collected herself. Fucking pulled herself together, trying hard to form a coherent thought as to what to do when she heard that voice again. Behind the wall was that room where she had checked the drive. Presumably Mia Winter’s room. And apparently, the woman had returned now. It meant that she was alive.

There were footsteps audible, they seemed erratic. Or… More than a pair? Was there someone else? But there was just one voice. It sounded exhausted. Weak. She assumed she had meanwhile fully come through because now she understood, could finally make sense of what she heard. "...I won't do it." Silence followed. Then the voice spoke again. "No." Another pause. There was no answer, however, no second voice. "Leave me alone." It was Mia. She was almost certain now.

She frowned. But who was she talking to? It didn’t seem like she was just rambling. There were interruptions, like she listened to something. "...you're not my daughter." Definitely Mia. Another silence. Then suddenly Mia’s voice broke into a plea. "No. Stop." The fear in those words immediately cut through her blur. It sounded so real. Mia was terrified. She thought about what she had read, about the hallucinations, about psychological control, about behavioral changes. Her brain throbbed, refusing cooperation. She didn’t allow it. Was the subject in the room? E-001? Was it there? Her own heart started to hammer but she breathed through. Get it together. So Mia was infected? Wasn’t unlikely. It happened to people who worked around biotechnologies. Mia had worked in connection with E-001 and now it had backfired. Bad luck. Could have happened to anybody. To her it had happened, too, in Spain. But she’d been lucky enough to obtain a cure in time. Taking from Mia’s notes she assumed that the subject most likely had also been the one who had infected the family that lived here and which perhaps had initially only wanted to help. They might have had nothing to do with the whole thing before. Now they were lost no matter what.

She flinched when a door slammed shut and a lock was turned. Had Mia left? Or had she been locked in? She saw the latter confirmed when she heard fists hammer against the door only seconds later. “Let me out. You won’t kill me anyway.” Then she went quiet as abruptly as she had flared up. Silence fell. And as distraction wore down she could feel the pain well up again, instinctively moved her halfway free hand toward her pocket, where she had a small collapsible canteen with water. She couldn’t reach it. She felt her throat turn sour, she would have wanted to cry. Her skin started to feel hot. Maybe a fever. Or maybe she imagined it. She honestly couldn't tell anymore.

It went dark around her, time passed. But she stayed present until she noticed something. A change, she just didn’t know in which way. It was quiet suddenly. Not just no one talking. But silent. There were no more footsteps. No voices. Mia, too, couldn’t be heard. Perhaps she had cried herself to sleep. She peaked through the hole into her room. It was empty. She tried to move slowly but every muscle screamed from remaining squeezed inside the cavity. She felt her breath hitch. Shit, she needed to get out of here. She needed to leave, now. Make a run for it, her ambitions of remaining undiscovered long faded to zero. All she wanted was to escape this mess.

She tried to move again as pain exploded through her head, spread through every fiber of her being. The world around her blurred as darkness rushed in from the edges of her vision. No. Not now. Please. She pushed with all her might, managed perhaps a few centimeters. Then everything vanished.

A scream that caused goosebumps to cascade all over her body dragged her back. "Ethan!" Her eyes snapped open and for a moment she didn’t know where she was. But memory returned mercilessly fast this time. It was lighter around her now, dawn was breaking. Then another scream came. "Ethan!" The sound tore through the house, yet nobody seemed to hear it. Or they simply ignored it. Mia didn’t stop. Screamed and screamed that name in pure exasperation. Then something shattered in the adjacent room. So hard that she felt the wall tremble against her shoulder that had meanwhile seemingly accustomed itself to the brick resting on it by turning entirely numb. A moment later she heard sobs. Broken sounds lacking any kind of hope. Desperate. Humane. She closed her eyes, didn’t know why it got to her so much suddenly. Could she have reached them, she would have wanted to cover her ears with her hands to keep it out. She tried to mentally mute herself instead.

Mia had gotten infected by either her own subject or by the one she had been dispatched to accompany during transport. So what? Playing with fire did not rarely get you burned. She was a collateral. She had lost. And yet… Why did Mia feel? Why was she still resisting? For this Ethan? Whoever he was? She couldn’t ever see him again anyway, could she? Why was she giving herself such a hard time when she could have resigned to her inevitable fate, spared herself the agitation? In her video she had told him to stay away herself. This was so pointless, wasn’t it?

And why did that bother her anyway? Who the hell was Mia Winters to her? A name, a room, she hadn’t even seen her. Just another pawn on the board. She was not her. Ethan wasn’t Leon. She swallowed, bit her lip and immediately tasted blood. Her thoughts had slipped. She tried to let go and failed and then she couldn’t stop it, not anymore, she was weak, couldn’t keep her defenses up, couldn’t help realizing that the truth was that it could have. And suddenly it cracked, hard, perhaps beyond repair, this armor that she wore so well. Because she was exhausted. Starved out, dehydrated and more dead than alive. And she suddenly no longer had the strength for denial. It slipped and left room for only one thought. Leon. Leon. Leon. Leon. Leon. Leon. She kept her mouth sealed, pressed her lips together but screamed his name regardless. He was as far as could be and yet she thought about the blood in her veins that she knew was his. A fact of that she was painfully aware whenever she got injured. When she hoped she would only lose her own and not what he had given her. Irrational as it was.

And she was trapped in here, like Mia. And filled her with some sort of compassion, some sort of sadness. Made her feel like she wanted to help her when she knew she couldn’t. Because it was what it was. Mia was a lost cause by every definition she had ever followed, she couldn’t be saved, she was a waste of time. She didn’t matter in the big picture. What mattered was the subject. Her mission. Everything else was just casualty. It was when her mind flickered to him again. She knew he wouldn’t have agreed. He didn’t think so much of the big picture. He didn’t serve the Government, not really. He served the people. He had dignity in the way he fought. Like the man in that book that he liked so much and that she had read for him once and often for herself since. He didn’t just deliver results, didn’t justify means by outcome. And over time, through shared battles and near-death moments, and a life closer or more distant alongside each other he had left something behind. Something that was more than just blood cells that had once been his and now lived within her.

He had not done intentionally so, of course not. He had never tried to change her, perhaps not dared, perhaps not wanted to. It was noticeable regardless. And it was more than just feeling for another person. Went far beyond a willingness to reveal a little about oneself and get to know his friends. Rather a change of perspective. For the better or for the worse. And she had never told him. So many things she had never entrusted him with when she trusted him to the bones, when she knew he longed for it so badly. Longed for her, longed for being let in at last. Why was it so fucking difficult? As if he would ever betray her. As if he would see any less in her when he knew who she really was.

Her head started to spin again, all these heavy thoughts exhausting her, sounds blurred into blood rushing. She would pass out again. Not long now. For the first time she truly considered the chance of not being able to make it out. Her muscles, her bones had long stopped hurting. She had become too weak for even that.

And she had always accepted death as a possibility. She had at all times more or less expected it. If she died in here, she didn’t think she’d regret anything. Not being dead itself. Not what she had done. Things had been as they had been. All of her decisions, every sin, every hardship in her life were parts of who she was. It got to her now regardless. Because she had always imagined herself facing it alone. She had never considered what it could mean to leave somebody behind. Somebody who mattered. Somebody she selfishly, recklessly had allowed herself to love. And so what plagued her now was not the possibility of dying but the insufferableness of never being able to speak to him again.

Sun had risen she realized on the reverie. It had become light all around her. How many days had she been in here? She didn’t know. And maybe it wasn’t of importance anymore. Her racing mind had worn her out entirely. And suddenly, all she wanted was to sleep. She submitted to the pull.

The crushing pain that had returned woke her back up with a start. Her shoulder now pounded heavily, her skull hammered, as if her body had decided to unleash one last burst of agony to drive her back into motion before finally giving up. She sucked in a sharp breath and immediately regretted it. But it made her feel alive. If she felt torment, she wasn’t dead.

She closed her eyes to listen carefully. There was nothing except a disturbing utter silence. It had gotten dark around her again. Perhaps the house was asleep. Did infected people really need sleep? She had no idea but she pushed her eyes back open, licked her dry lips. She needed water. So badly she found it hard to even think about it. She wouldn’t make it another day, she knew. She couldn’t reach the emergency bar she had brought, couldn’t reach the small collapsible canteen in her pocket, it was too tight in here. This was her last chance. She had to take it or she would be lost.

Her neck screamed when she tried to move. One of her legs had gone completely numb. Her shoulder felt as though somebody was driving a knife through the joint. But to her own surprise, she remained conscious. Clearer than she had been since the brick had hit her. Adrenaline, she suspected. She needed to utilize it now. Because she wouldn’t get another opportunity. Okay then. Move out, focus on escaping. She drew a slow breath. And then pushed towards the small opening. The narrow cavity seemed determined to keep her, however, made her struggle, writhe, ache and for a moment she thought about giving up. Why go through this hell when she could just sleep, just fade away?

No. She. Would. Not. Die. In. Here.

She pushed again, felt edges scrape her clothes, got stuck. Her heart skipped a beat. She tried to withdraw, realized she couldn't go backward anymore. If she stayed that way though, she would be discovered at the next opportunity. So it had to be forward now and she pressed harder, with all her might, twisted her shoulders.

And was finally released.

She collapsed onto the floor with a low, clumsy thud and for several seconds remained there, frozen, breathing through the dizziness and listening for any signs of movement. Her whole body tingled as her blood circulation resumed. When the numbness slowly faded, she sat up. Before she knew it, she reached for the water with shaky fingers and drowned it on one heavy gulp. Her throat protested and she coughed, pressed her hands over her mouth hard to muffle the noise. The liquid burned like acid. It fired in her lungs. But she forced herself to continue, forced herself to drink up, even though a very fair share got lost on the way.

She was starved too, but not here. Not a necessity. Everything hurt but she couldn’t pay attention to that either. She could move all of her extremities. And that was all that mattered for the moment. She noticed moonlight through the shabby curtains. Must be the middle of the night. She tucked her little canteen that now more looked like a mere piece of fabric anymore back into her pocket and struggled up. The soles of her feet tickled unpleasantly. But she started towards the door immediately. And despite her condition, her body remembered everything she had learned for years by heart, moved cautiously and measuredly and surprisingly steadily. She opened the door without a sound found the hallway behind it deserted. Clear. Downstairs now and out. But she froze when her gaze got caught at Mia’s room. The key was in the lock.

She closed her eyes briefly. Fucking leave this place. While you still can. It was a miracle already that she was able to even stand upright. But she couldn’t move, held her breath to listen instead. Not a sound. Absolute silence. She couldn’t bring herself to proceed. Something commanded her to at least take a look. And so she went towards Mia’s door and put her ear to the wood. She heard nothing except for the thrumming of her own heart. Was Mia really infected? Would it be visible? Her hand found its way to the key all by itself and before she knew she turned it. It would only be a minute. She would just sneak inside, confirm her condition and then immediately leave. She placed her hand against the doorframe and listened one last time. Then she drew in a deep breath, unlocked the door and slid inside.

It was quiet in here. The room was softly illuminated by the moonlight. She went to Mia’s bedside to look at her sleeping form, took her in. She looked bad. Barely recognizable from the photos she had seen. Worse than she had anticipated. Bloodied. Worn down. Yet breathing softly. Still humane. Which didn’t make sense. But only increased the compassion she already felt. For the way she had cried for this Ethan. The way she had whimpered for him, had called his name over and over. Ethan. Ethan. Ethan. Ethan. Ethan. Ethan. She briefly surveyed her body once again, perhaps hoping she’d been mistaken. But she hadn’t. This woman was gray as the house, fading away like vegetation that was slowly being swallowed up by swamps. She was a lost cause. She couldn’t help her. She had to leave now. Or…? No. Walk away.

She didn’t walk away. Instead, she carefully took the phone she carried from her pocket and opened it. She undid the small SIM card, went to the desk and placed it underneath the data stick. Safety and anonymity had always been her priority. The card had been prepaid. She had never used it before coming to this place. It couldn’t be traced back to anything. She had never once made a call from that number nor sent a message. She had ordered it online and picked up from a public deposit years ago. It didn’t provide a large data volume.
